observation_id: obs_philebus_0001

  text_sha256: c327d91fec13b363b56ecbaf0871400ba26f83c4b50dcbec5d72665bbf3d3167
greek_terms: []
english_gloss: ""
feature_family: dramatic_case_setup
feature_id: feature_candidate_1431
feature_label: competing_thesis_announced
observation: "The dialogue opens with Socrates summarizing two competing theses: Philebus claims that pleasure (to chairein), enjoyment, and delight are the good for all living beings; Socrates maintains that intelligence, thought, memory, right opinion, and true calculations are superior to pleasure and most beneficial for all beings capable of sharing in them (11a-11c)."
textual_basis: "Socrates explicitly recapitulates (sygkephalaiōsometha) the two positions at 11b, naming Philebus's thesis about pleasure and his own counter-thesis about phronēsis. The competing candidates for the good are set out before the investigation begins."
limits: "This observation records the opening dramatic setup and the explicit statement of opposing theses. It does not assess the merits of either thesis or track how the positions evolve later in the dialogue."
review_status: accepted
```

feature_id: feature_candidate_2583
feature_label: element_assigned_to_classificatory_genus
observation: "At 27e, Socrates asks which previously stated genus contains pleasure and pain, and Philebus places them among things admitting the more and less rather than among things with limit."
textual_basis: "Socrates frames the question as a genus-placement problem and asks whether pleasure and pain have limit or belong to things receiving more and less. Philebus answers that they belong to the more, and adds that pleasure would not be every good unless it were by nature unlimited in multitude and degree."
limits: "This observation records the classification move at 27e. It does not assess whether Philebus's reason succeeds or how Socrates will later use the placement."
review_status: accepted
```

observation: "Socrates both proposes the recapitulation (boulei sygkephalaiōsometha, 11b) and delivers both sides of the dispute himself. Philebus's thesis is stated by Socrates, not by Philebus, and Philebus merely assents (pantōn men oun malista, 11c)."
textual_basis: "At 11a-11b Socrates asks Protarchus what argument he will receive from Philebus and proposes a joint summary. He then states both positions in full (11b-11c). Philebus's only speech in the segment is a brief assent at 11c, after his own position has already been articulated for him."
limits: "This records Socrates's procedural control at the outset. It does not track how turn-taking evolves across the dialogue or whether Protarchus later asserts procedural agency."
review_status: accepted
```

feature_label: contest_framed_as_psyche_disposition
observation: Socrates frames the inquiry as a contest between two sides, each tasked with exhibiting a disposition and condition of soul capable of providing a happy life to all humans (11d). Socrates and his side champion phronesis, while Protarchus and his side champion enjoyment.
textual_basis: At 11d Socrates proposes that each side will attempt to display a disposition and condition of soul that can furnish a happy life. He then identifies his side with phronein and theirs with chairein, setting the inquiry as a contest.
limits: This observation notes the framing of the inquiry as a contest over soul-dispositions, not the outcome of that contest or any later psychological argument.
review_status: accepted
```

textual_basis: At 12a Protarchus says to Philebus that having handed over the argument to us, you would no longer be in authority over the agreement with Socrates or even the opposite. observation: Socrates proposes a victory condition for the contest at 11e-12a: if a third life appears superior to both pleasure and phronesis, the life more akin to pleasure defeats phronesis, and vice versa.
textual_basis: At 11e-12a Socrates states that if some other life appears stronger than both, then if it appears more akin to pleasure, the life of pleasure conquers that of phronesis; if it appears more akin to phronesis, phronesis conquers pleasure.
limits: This observation notes only the stated procedural victory condition. It does not analyze whether the third-life scenario materializes, how kinship is determined, or what this implies for the contest's fairness.
review_status: accepted
```

feature_id: feature_candidate_1439
feature_label: one_over_many_problem_introduced
observation: "Socrates introduces the one-over-many problem for pleasure: pleasure is heard as one thing (hen ti) but takes manifold forms (morphas pantioas) that are in some way unlike each other (anomoious allelais)."
textual_basis: "At 12c Socrates says pleasure, though heard simply as one thing (hen ti), has taken all kinds of forms (morphas pantioas) and is in some way unlike itself (tina tropon anomoious allelais). This sets up the problem of how a single thing can have multiple unlike forms, using the vocabulary of unity (hen) and form (morphe)."
limits: "This observation records the introduction of the one-and-many problem in the context of pleasure. It does not claim this is a Form in the technical sense of the middle-period dialogues, only that the vocabulary of unity and multiplicity is explicitly deployed."
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_philebus_0010
source_work: Philebus
stephanus_span: 12d-12e
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/philebus.txt
  stephanus_span: 12d-12e
  start_marker: 12d
  end_marker: 12e
  start_char: 2662
  end_char: 3442
  text_sha256: 6fedb1f9d96af7ce86418841ba4d96fc4ca311f8d34a903138b195652cdaa287
greek_terms:
  - ἀκολασταίνοντα
  - σωφρονοῦντα
  - ἀνοηταίνοντα
  - φρονοῦντα
english_gloss: ""
feature_family: elenchus
feature_id: feature_candidate_1440
feature_label: opposing_states_produce_opposed_affections
observation: |
  Socrates builds two parallel contrasts to argue that pleasures cannot all be homogeneous: the intemperate person and the moderate person both feel pleasure, and the foolish person and the prudent person both feel pleasure, yet in each pair the pleasures arise from opposed conditions. Protarchus concedes that the pleasures arise from opposite sources (12e) but denies that the pleasures themselves are opposites.
textual_basis: |
  At 12d Socrates lists four character types in two contrasting pairs (intemperate/moderate, foolish/prudent), asserting each experiences pleasure. He then asks how anyone who calls each pair's pleasures alike would not rightly appear foolish. This structures an elenctic challenge: Protarchus must either accept that pleasures differ based on their source or maintain that all pleasures are generically alike.
limits: |
  This observation records the argument structure and Protarchus's initial resistance at 12e. It does not assess whether the argument succeeds or whether Protarchus's distinction between source-contrariety and pleasure-contrariety is ultimately sustainable.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_philebus_0011
source_work: Philebus
stephanus_span: 12e
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/philebus.txt
  stephanus_span: 12e
  start_marker: 12e
  end_marker: 12e
  start_char: 3069
  end_char: 3442
  text_sha256: e698d04f47979e2d53965e12df4b810c1f7ba4ba797a9558b964b554e81fb203
greek_terms:
  - χρῶμα χρώματι
  - μέλαν τῷ λευκῷ
  - σχῆμα σχήματι
  - γένει μέν ἐστι πᾶν ἕν
  - τὰ δὲ μέρη τοῖς μέρεσιν
english_gloss: ""
feature_family: elenchus
feature_id: feature_candidate_1441
feature_label: genus_parts_analogy_refutation
observation: |
  Socrates refutes Protarchus's claim that pleasure must be most like pleasure by invoking color and shape: each is one as a genus, yet its parts can be different and opposed.
textual_basis: |
  At 12e Socrates answers the generic identity claim with two counterexamples: color is color, but black and white are recognized as opposite; shape is likewise one in genus while its parts differ from one another. The analogy blocks the inference from one genus-name to homogeneous parts.
limits: |
  This records the analogy structure and its refutational function. It does not include the preceding list of opposed pleasures or evaluate whether pleasure is divided exactly as color and shape are.
review_status: accepted
```

feature_label: mock_exasperation_address
observation: |
  At 12e Socrates addresses Protarchus with the vocative translated as "you wondrous fellow" immediately before deploying the color and shape analogies. This vocative marks a tonal shift: Socrates treats Protarchus's position as willfully obtuse while maintaining the argumentative surface.
textual_basis: |
  At 12e Socrates opens the analogy refutation with the address that begins the color-to-color comparison. The vocative signals exasperation or mock-wonder at a claim Socrates finds obviously mistaken. It appears at the pivot from Protarchus's defense to Socrates's decisive counter-argument.
limits: |
  This records the presence of a marked vocative at a refutational pivot. It does not claim that Socrates's entire demeanor is ironic or that the argument that follows is insincere. The vocative can carry a range of tones from affectionate rebuke to genuine wonder.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_philebus_0013
source_work: Philebus
stephanus_span: 13a-13b
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/philebus.txt
  stephanus_span: 13a-13b
  start_marker: 13a
  end_marker: 13b
  start_char: 3442
  end_char: 4278
  text_sha256: 7fe2a166fc3b81661dab6e3142812d6341189cb3024bd554999e02f6d77b01c4
greek_terms:
  - ἐναντιώτατα
  - ταὐτὸν
english_gloss: ""
feature_family: forms_trajectory
feature_id: feature_candidate_1499
feature_label: one_over_many_demand
observation: >-
  At 13b Socrates demands Protarchus identify what identical element is present equally in bad and good pleasures that justifies calling all pleasures good. This follows from the observation at 13a that some pleasures may be opposite to others. The demand assumes that a single predicate applied across unlike instances requires a common element in those instances.
textual_basis: >-
  Socrates explicitly asks: what then is the same thing present in both bad pleasures and good ones equally such that you address all pleasures as good (13b). The question presupposes that calling unlike things by the same name requires some identical feature present in all of them.
limits: >-
  The passage does not use form-language such as eidos or idea. The demand is for a common element, not an assertion that one exists. The observation records the structure of the demand, not a metaphysical commitment.
review_status: accepted
```

observation: >-
  Socrates exposes a tension in Protarchus's commitments: Protarchus agrees that pleasures are unlike one another but calls them all good by a single name. At 13b Socrates notes that Protarchus admits pleasures are unlike yet applies one predicate good to them all. The move treats this combination of admissions as inconsistent without an intervening account of the common element.
textual_basis: >-
  At 13a Socrates says Protarchus calls unlike things by another name. At 13b he restates: Protarchus agrees pleasures are unlike yet calls all of them good.
limits: >-
  Protarchus has not yet conceded a contradiction; the passage ends with his resistance at 13b. The observation records the structure of the challenge, not its resolution.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_philebus_0016
source_work: Philebus
stephanus_span: 13c-13d
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/philebus.txt
  stephanus_span: 13c-13d
  start_marker: 13c
  end_marker: 13d
  start_char: 4278
  end_char: 5069
  text_sha256: 55c31301acd40ff412ff371593b1d19784e7662d4479cf52eae6a10ed49d2f40
greek_terms: ["ἀνομοίους", "ἡδονὴν ἡδονῆς διάφορον", "πάσας ὁμοίας"]
english_gloss: "pleasures unlike, pleasure differing from pleasure, all alike"
feature_family: elenchus
feature_id: feature_candidate_380
feature_label: forced_alternative
observation: "Socrates presses Protarchus into a dilemma: if pleasures are not unlike and opposed, they must all be alike; but if some are unlike, the argument collapses into a juvenile mirroring game."
textual_basis: "At 13c Protarchus resists conceding that pleasures are opposed qua pleasures. Socrates responds at 13c-13d by rephrasing the denial into the claim that all pleasures are alike (pasas homoias), then warns that if he mirrors Protarchus's move — treating the most unlike as most like — the argument will slip away and be gone. He proposes to pull back (anakrouometha) and try again."
limits: "This records the dilemma structure and the explicit threat of argumentative collapse; it does not claim that Socrates endorses either horn or that a contradiction is formally derived."
review_status: accepted
```

textual_basis: "At 13c-d Socrates says those who hold that all pleasures are alike because none differ qua pleasure are like the most worthless men and young in arguments (hoi panton phaulotatoi te kai peri logous hama neoi). limits: "This does not claim that youth or worthlessness is a formal refutation. It records the ad hominem characterization of a dialectical stance."
review_status: accepted
```

limits: "This records the explicit procedural reset language. It does not analyze whether the reset is successful or fair to Protarchus."
review_status: accepted
```

feature_label: reductio_ad_absurdum_hypothetical
observation: Socrates deploys a hypothetical reductio: if he were to deny that knowledges are many and unlike one another out of fear, the argument would perish like a myth and they would be saved only by clinging to something irrational.
textual_basis: At 13e-14a Socrates asks whether the many knowledges are unlike and even in some way opposed to one another, then poses the conditional: if he denied any unlikeness among knowledges, the argument would vanish like a myth and they would be saved on some irrational ground (14a).
limits: The observation records a hypothetical reductio structure; it does not claim that Socrates endorses the truth of the counterfactual or that an actual contradiction has been demonstrated.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_philebus_0022

  - πολλά
  - ἄπειρα
english_gloss: "one and many, unlimited"
feature_family: forms_trajectory
feature_id: feature_candidate_1439
feature_label: one_over_many_problem_introduced
observation: "Socrates distinguishes between a trivial, widely-accepted version of the one-many problem and a more serious version that has not yet been conceded. The trivial version involves sensible things being both one and many (e.g., a person being large and small)."
textual_basis: "Socrates, responding to Protarchus at 14d-14e, calls the examples Protarchus gave the common currency of wonders about the one and many, already conceded by nearly everyone. He contrasts them with the case where someone, having divided a thing into its limbs and parts, agrees that all those parts are that one thing, and is then refuted by being forced to say that the one is many and unlimited, and the many are only one."
limits: "The passage does not specify what the more serious one-many problem is; it merely establishes that a boundary exists between a conceded version and an unconceded version. The serious version is not yet defined in this span."
review_status: accepted
```

observation: "Socrates proposes that he and Protarchus set aside their initial positions on whether pleasure or phronesis is the good, lay out their differences openly, and let examination reveal which position wins — explicitly subordinating personal victory to alliance with what is truest."
textual_basis: "At 14b Socrates states they should not conceal the difference between his and Protarchus's account of the good but place it in the open; he disclaims that either side's view should win merely qua theirs and insists both must fight alongside what is truest. Protarchus assents."
limits: "This is a procedural commitment, not a resolution of the substantive issue. The passage does not indicate what procedure will follow or how the truest will be identified."

```yaml
observation_id: obs_philebus_0027
source_work: Philebus
stephanus_span: 15c-15d

  text_sha256: b7f83368a092c48bb63992966cc90666607391d1d5b9d7a809c1f239e36c15b8
greek_terms:
  - ἓν καὶ πολλά
  - ἀπορίας
  - εὐπορίας
english_gloss: ""
feature_family: elenchus
feature_id: feature_candidate_1623
feature_label: aporia_marked_as_agreement_failure
observation: |
  Socrates identifies the one-many problem as the source of aporia or euporia depending on whether it is properly agreed upon (homologethenta), not on whether it is intrinsically resolvable.
textual_basis: |
  At 15c Socrates says the one-and-many (hen kai polla) of such things are 'the cause of all aporia if not well agreed upon and conversely of euporia if well agreed upon' (hapases aporias aitia me kalos homologethenta kai euporias ... au kalos). The contrast between aporia and euporia is tied to the quality of agreement (homologethenta) rather than to the nature of the problem itself.
limits: |
  The observation records Socrates' framing of the one-many problem's difficulty as procedural rather than ontological. It does not claim that all aporia in the dialogue reduces to agreement failure, nor does it analyze whether the agreement condition is satisfied in what follows.
review_status: accepted
```

  Protarchus proposes to leave Philebus undisturbed ('not to move him since he lies well'), effectively removing the dialogue's namesake from the inquiry at 15c.
textual_basis: |
  At 15c Protarchus says Philebos is best left alone in the present inquiry: 'Philebon d' isos kratiston en to nun eperotonta me kinein eu keimenon' (it is perhaps best not to disturb Philebus by questioning him now, since he lies well). This explicitly sidelines the title character as an active interlocutor.
limits: |
  This observation records a dramatic fact about interlocutor management. It does not interpret Philebus's silence as having any hidden meaning, nor does it claim the dialogue's argument would differ if Philebus participated.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_philebus_0029
source_work: Philebus

greek_terms:
  - ἀθάνατόν τι καὶ ἀγήρων πάθος
  - τῶν λόγων αὐτῶν
english_gloss: ""
feature_family: myth_demarcation
feature_id: feature_candidate_1625
feature_label: argumentative_pattern_called_immortal
observation: |
  Socrates describes the one-many problem as 'an immortal and ageless affection of the logoi themselves in us' (15d), using language that ascribes permanence to a dialectical pattern rather than to a divine myth.
textual_basis: |
  At 15d Socrates says 'touto oute me pausetai pote oute erxato nun, all' esti to toiouton, hos emoi phainetai, ton logon auton athanaton ti kai ageron pathos en hemin.' The one-many perplexity is said never to cease, never to have begun now, and to be an immortal and ageless affection belonging to the logoi themselves. The language of immortality (athanaton) and agelessness (ageron) is typically reserved for divine or mythic entities, but here it is applied to a recurring argumentative experience.
limits: |
  The observation notes the register of the language without claiming that Socrates is deploying a myth or that the passage constitutes a myth/logos boundary. It does not assert that the description is literally true or that it has metaphysical implications.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_philebus_0032

greek_terms:
  - νέοι πάντες ἐσμέν
  - μή σοι μετὰ Φιλήβου συνεπιθώμεθα
english_gloss: "we are all young ... lest we join with Philebus in attacking you"
feature_family: turn_geometry
feature_id: feature_candidate_720
feature_label: explicit_procedure_control
observation: "Protarchus responds to Socrates' self-description by noting that the audience is all young and threatening that they might join with Philebus in attacking Socrates if he insults them. He couples this threat with a procedural request for a way and device to exit the confusion and find a path forward in the argument."
textual_basis: "At 16a Protarchus asks whether Socrates sees that the group is all young, and warns that they might join Philebus in attacking him if he abuses them. He immediately adds that they do understand what Socrates is saying and asks for a method and device to depart from the confusion in a kindly way and find a road forward. The threat is playful but functions as a procedural check on Socrates' digression."
limits: "Does not claim the threat is serious; it may be ironic or playful. Does not claim the method requested here is identical to the one eventually delivered later in the dialogue."
review_status: accepted
```

feature_id: feature_candidate_1679
feature_label: gift_of_gods_narrative
observation: Socrates introduces the method of one-and-many as a gift from the gods to men, thrown down through some Prometheus together with blazing fire, and transmitted by the ancients who were greater and nearer to the gods.
textual_basis: At 16c Socrates says "theōn men eis anthrōpous dosis" (gift of the gods to men), thrown down "dia tinos Promētheōs hama phanotatōi tini pyri" (through some Prometheus with most blazing fire), and that the ancients (hoi palaioi), who were "kreittones hēmōn kai eggyterō theōn oikountes" (greater than us and dwelling nearer to the gods), handed down the teaching that things said to be are from one and many and have limit and unlimitedness (peras kai apeirian) inherent in them.
limits: This observation notes the mythic framing of a methodological claim. It does not evaluate the truth of the one-and-many doctrine, nor does it classify the Prometheus reference as allegory or historical allusion.
review_status: accepted
```

textual_basis: At 16b Socrates says "ouk esti kalliōn hodos oud' an genoito hēs egō erastēs men eimi aei, pollakis de me ēdē diaphygousa erēmon kai aporon katestēsen" (16b). This is a first-person declaration of methodological desire and recurrent failure, prompted by Protarchus's request that the method be stated.
limits: This observation records the erotic language for method and the admission of failure. It does not claim that the passage enacts aporia as a dramatic device, nor does it link this to elenctic practice elsewhere.

  - μονάδας
  - ἑνάδων
english_gloss: ""
feature_family: forms_trajectory
feature_id: feature_candidate_1439
feature_label: one_over_many_problem_introduced
observation: >
  Socrates frames the core one-over-many aporia as a three-part inquiry at 15b.
  He asks first whether such monads should be supposed to truly exist, then how
  each can remain always one and the same while nonetheless being most securely
  one, and finally whether in generated and unlimited things the one should be
  posited as dispersed into many or as a whole separate from itself.
textual_basis: >
  At 15b Socrates enumerates three successive questions (first... then... then...)
  that structure the inquiry about henads and monads. The third question explicitly
  poses the alternative between the one being dispersed and many (diespasmenen kai
  polla) or whole apart from itself (holen auten hautes choris), calling the
  latter the most impossible scenario of all.
limits: >
  The observation records that the problem is posed as a structured inquiry at
  15a-15b. It does not assess whether the questions are answered later in the
  dialogue.
review_status: accepted
```

feature_label: participation_whole_part_dilemma
observation: >
  At 15b Socrates poses an explicit dilemma about how the one participates in
  the many: either it is to be posited as dispersed and having become many in
  generated and unlimited things, or as a whole separate from itself. He
  characterizes the latter horn — that the same and one could be at once in one
  and in many — as the most impossible thing of all.
textual_basis: >
  The text at 15b presents two alternatives for how the one is to be posited
  (theteon) in generated things: as dispersed and many (diespasmenen kai polla
  gegonuian) or as whole apart from itself (holen auten hautes choris). Socrates
  adds that the latter would appear most impossible of all (panton
  adynatotaton), since it would require the same and one to be simultaneously
  in one and in many.
limits: >
  The observation records the dilemma as posed at 15b. It does not trace how
  this dilemma is resolved elsewhere in the dialogue.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_philebus_0043

greek_terms:
  - σοφός
  - ἔμφρων
  - φρονεῖν
  - ἐλλόγιμον
  - ἐνάριθμον
english_gloss: "wise, intelligent, understanding, rational, counted"
feature_family: definition_ladder
feature_id: feature_candidate_1164
feature_label: criterion_for_evaluative_classification
observation: >
  Socrates makes sound understanding depend on looking to a determinate
  number rather than to the unlimited multitude. He correlates being
  wise and intelligent with grasping the one through number, and opposes
  these to being without understanding, without rational account, and
  unnumbered when one remains with the unlimited.
textual_basis: >
  At 17e the vocabulary of cognitive classification is explicit:
  one becomes wise (sophos) when grasping the one through number, and
  intelligent (emphron) regarding a thing; the unlimited multitude
  makes one lack understanding (phronein) and renders one without
  rational account (ellogimon) and unnumbered (enarithmon). These
  paired oppositions establish a criterion: sound cognition requires
  number and limit; their absence marks defective cognition.
limits: >
  The passage does not apply this criterion to any specific subject
  matter; it states the criterion in general methodological terms.
  It does not distinguish kinds of knowledge or degrees of wisdom.
review_status: accepted
```

  At 18a-18b Socrates states the reciprocal rule for one and the unlimited:

  likewise from the unlimited back toward one through determinate number.

feature_label: humble_craft_as_methodological_paradigm
observation: "Socrates presents the invention of the alphabet (grammar/letters) as a methodological paradigm for the dialectical procedure of finding the one and the many: first grasp a number/plurality within the unlimited, then proceed to the one."
textual_basis: "At 18b-c Socrates explicitly connects the dialectical procedure from 18b (not going straight to the one but first grasping some number) to letters (grammata). He narrates how Theuth perceived that vocal sound (phone) is unlimited, distinguished vowels, then a third class of letters (aphona/voiceless), divided each down to the individual unit, and gave each and all the name 'element' (stoicheion). This technical analysis of a humble craft domain serves as a model for the philosophical method under discussion."
limits: "The observation notes the methodological use of the alphabet invention story. It does not assess whether the Theuth story is historical, nor does it claim this is the only methodological paradigm in the dialogue."
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml

greek_terms: []
english_gloss: ""
feature_family: turn_geometry
feature_id: feature_candidate_720
feature_label: explicit_procedure_control
observation: |
  Protarchus declares that Socrates has posed a non-trivial question and calls on Philebus to decide which of them should answer, then rules out the absurd outcome that neither could answer, and reframes what Socrates is asking — whether pleasure has forms, how many, and of what sort, and the same for phronesis.
textual_basis: |
  At 19a Protarchus says the question is not trivial (ouk eis phaulon ge erotema), describes Socrates as having led them in a circle, and asks Philebus to decide who answers. At 19b he states it would be ridiculous if neither could answer and then articulates the inquiry agenda: forms of pleasure and phronesis.
limits: |
  This observation records the procedural turn-management and agenda-reframing, not the content of Socrates's prior argument or the eventual outcome of the inquiry.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml

greek_terms: []
english_gloss: ""
feature_family: turn_geometry
feature_id: feature_candidate_720
feature_label: explicit_procedure_control
observation: |
  Protarchus, having taken over as respondent from Philebus, now calls on Philebus to decide who should answer Socrates' current question, thereby expressing reluctance to carry the respondent role forward alone.
textual_basis: |
  At 19a Protarchus addresses Philebus and says "consider which of us shall answer the present question" (skopei de poteros hemon apokrineitai to nun erotomenon), then acknowledges it would be absurd for him, having become successor in the argument (ton logou diadochon), to pass the question back to Philebus simply because he cannot answer it.
limits: |
  This records the turn-transfer dynamic and the respondent's hesitation, not the philosophical content of the question. It does not claim Protarchus permanently exits the role.
review_status: accepted
```

observation: |
  Socrates endorses Protarchus's reframing of the inquiry and asserts that failing to grasp the forms (eide) of pleasure and phronesis per each one, its like, its same, and its opposite renders a person worthless for anything.
textual_basis: |
  At 19b Socrates calls Protarchus's account truest (alethestata legeis, o pai Kalliou) and states that whoever cannot do this — i.e., determine the forms for each one, the like, the same, and the opposite, as the preceding argument indicated — would never be worth anything for anything (oudeis eis ouden oudenos an hemon oudepote genoito axios).
limits: |
  This records the procedural commitment to form-based inquiry as a criterion of worth; it does not evaluate whether Socrates is sincere or whether the criterion is subsequently met.
review_status: accepted
```

  knowledge, understanding, craft and their kin are better than pleasure.

  At 19c-d, Protarchus restates Philebus's original position (hedone, terpsis,
  chara) and Socrates's counter-position (nous, episteme, synesis, techne) as the
  two sides of the amphisbetesis (dispute). This is an explicit recapitulation of
  the argumentative stakes before a procedural transition.

english_gloss: ""
feature_family: definition_ladder
feature_id: feature_candidate_1164
feature_label: criterion_for_evaluative_classification
observation: Socrates proposes three criteria the good must satisfy — being complete (teleon), sufficient (hikanon), and such that every knowing being hunts and desires it — then uses the first two criteria to set up a comparative test of the life of pleasure and the life of phronesis.
textual_basis: At 20d Socrates asks whether the good's portion must be complete (teleon) and sufficient (hikanon), obtaining Protarchus's assent. He adds that every knowing being hunts the good (thēreuei kai ephietai). At 20e he deploys the completeness criterion as a decision procedure: if either the pleasure life or the phronesis life is the good, it must need nothing further (mēden mēdenos eti prosdeisthai). The two lives are then to be examined separately (chōris).
limits: This observation records the criteria Socrates states and his procedural move to test the two candidate lives against them. It does not evaluate whether the criteria themselves are justified or whether the test is fairly applied.
review_status: accepted
```

observation: Socrates announces a joint examination procedure (skopōmen dē kai krinōmen) in which the lives of pleasure and phronesis will be viewed separately, and Protarchus signals confusion (pōs eipes;) requiring the procedure to be restated and clarified.
textual_basis: At 20e Socrates says "let us examine and judge" the life of pleasure and the life of phronesis "having viewed them separately" (idontes chōris). He then states the procedural condition: if either is the good it must need nothing further. Protarchus's "pōs eipes?" (how do you mean?) prompts Socrates to restate the separability condition explicitly: neither phronesis in the pleasure life nor pleasure in the phronesis life.
limits: This records the procedural framing and the interlocutor's momentary non-comprehension. It does not assess whether the separability condition is sound or whether Protarchus's confusion is genuine.
review_status: accepted
```

feature_label: assent_chain
observation: Protarchus assents rapidly to three consecutive Socratic claims about the good — that it is most complete, sufficient, and that every knowing being pursues it — culminating in the blanket assent "it is impossible to contradict these points" (ouk esti toutois anteipein).
textual_basis: At 20d Socrates asks whether the good's portion is complete; Protarchus replies "most complete of all surely" (pantōn dēpou teleōtaton). Asked whether the good is sufficient, he replies "how could it not be?" (pōs gar ou;). After the hunting claim he says "it is impossible to contradict these points" (ouk esti toutois anteipein). Three rapid assents in succession, the last a global one.
limits: This records the assent pattern. It does not claim Protarchus has been refuted or that his assents are unwarranted.
review_status: accepted
```

observation: "Protarchus explicitly identifies Socrates' dialectical method as one that throws interlocutors into impasse through questioning they cannot adequately answer, and he refuses to accept this as the end of the present inquiry."
textual_basis: "At 20a Protarchus tells Socrates to stop throwing them into impasse and cross-questioning them on matters they might not be able to give an adequate answer to at present. He adds that the impasse of all of them should not be the end, and that Socrates himself must do the work since he promised."
limits: "This records a respondent diagnosing and preemptively rejecting a familiar Socratic tactic of producing aporia through questioning; it does not establish whether this description of Socratic method is accurate or fair, nor does it evaluate the philosophical merits of the impasse itself."
review_status: accepted
```

He asks Protarchus whether he would accept a life consisting entirely of the greatest pleasures, then probes whether anything further — specifically cognitive faculties — would still be needed.
textual_basis: At 21a-b Socrates poses a series of short questions beginning with a prompt about living one's whole life enjoying the greatest pleasures (21a). After Protarchus denies needing anything else, Socrates introduces the question of cognitive goods: phronesis, nous, reckoning, memory, knowledge, and true opinion (21a-21b). The scenario is a thought-experiment, not an argument from prior commitments.

feature_label: assent_chain
observation: Socrates secures a rapid series of assents from Protarchus: (1) he would accept a life of pure pleasure, (2) he would need nothing further, (3) he would not miss cognitive faculties. Socrates then derives a consequence: without cognitive faculties, the person living such a life would necessarily be ignorant of whether he is actually experiencing pleasure, since he is empty of all phronesis.
textual_basis: The exchange at 21a-b moves through short questions and affirmations (with Protarchus's quick assents at 21a) culminating in Socrates' conclusion that without nous, memory, knowledge, and true opinion one would be ignorant of one's own pleasure (21b). Protarchus assents to this conclusion as necessary at 21b.
limits: This record notes the assent-chain structure and the conclusion that pure pleasure without cognition entails ignorance of pleasure. It does not claim that this refutes hedonism or that Protarchus is inconsistent.
review_status: accepted
```

textual_basis: After Protarchus denies needing anything beyond the greatest pleasures (21a), Socrates immediately asks whether he would still need phronesis, nous, reckoning, and related cognitive goods (21a-21b), framing the inquiry as a test (using the term for testing, 21a).   text_sha256: 879a408bb868e8903e2eb0a84785052350623585391d9176cadcd2f3c9466439
greek_terms: []
english_gloss: ""
feature_family: closure_type
feature_id: feature_candidate_1992
feature_label: ranked_prize_closure
observation: |
  Socrates concludes the section by assigning a rank order: intellect is more akin to first and second prize, while pleasure is said to fall short even of third prize (22e). Protarchus confirms the image of pleasure's defeat: pleasure has fallen as if struck down, lies beaten while contesting for the victory prizes (22e).
textual_basis: |
  At 22e Socrates states that on this argument pleasure would never truly partake of first or second prize, and is farther off than third prize. Protarchus at 22e confirms the defeat image using contest vocabulary.
limits: |
  This records the ranked-prize closure form and Protarchus's confirmation of the defeat image. It does not evaluate whether the argument preceding this passage justifies the ranking.
review_status: accepted
```

english_gloss: "sufficient, complete, choiceworthy"
feature_family: definition_ladder
feature_id: feature_candidate_2014
feature_label: criteria_triad_applied_to_candidate
observation: |
  At 22b Socrates applies a three-criterion test to reject both the life of pleasure alone and the life of intelligence alone. The terms applied are "sufficient," "complete," and "choiceworthy" for all plants and animals capable of living that way through life. Neither candidate passes, so neither is the good.
textual_basis: |
  At 22b Socrates states that if either of the two lives had possessed the good, it would have been sufficient and complete and choiceworthy for all plants and animals capable of living that way through life. The explicit listing of three criteria and their application to the two candidate lives constitutes a definitional testing procedure.
limits: |
  This observation records the application of the three criteria at this juncture. It does not assess whether these criteria are the only ones Socrates employs in the Philebus, nor does it claim that the triad is formally defined as a set elsewhere.
review_status: accepted
```

english_gloss: "goddess, intelligence, the true and divine intelligence"
feature_family: definition_ladder
feature_id: feature_candidate_2015
feature_label: candidate_eliminated_from_contest
observation: |
  At 22c Socrates declares that the Phileban goddess is not to be thought identical with the good, and has been sufficiently shown to be so. Philebus retorts that Socrates' intelligence is equally not the good and faces the same charges. Socrates concedes regarding his own intelligence but distinguishes the true and divine intelligence, which he says is otherwise situated.
textual_basis: |
  At 22c Socrates says that the Phileban goddess must not be thought the same as the good and that this has been sufficiently stated. Philebus immediately claims Socrates' intelligence is equally not the good and will face the same charges. Socrates concedes for his own intelligence but not for the true and divine intelligence.
limits: |
  This observation records the elimination statement and the symmetry objection. It does not evaluate whether Socrates' distinction between his own intelligence and the divine intelligence is a valid move, nor does it track the later fate of this distinction.
review_status: accepted
```

This confirms that the inquiry is framed as a prize-awarding contest with ranked outcomes.
textual_basis: |
  At 22c Socrates uses prize terminology explicitly, placing victory prizes and second prizes in relation to the mixed life. He defers the first-prize question and directs attention to second prizes, implying a ranked contest framework that had been established earlier in the dialogue.

observation: >
  Socrates frames the continuation of the argument as a contest for second prize (ta deutereia). The metaphor is introduced at 23a with the remark that pleasure has been deprived of the second prize, and Socrates returns to it at 23b when he says he must go after the second prize on behalf of nous.
textual_basis: >
  At 23a Socrates says pleasure, having been deprived of the second prize, would suffer dishonor among its own lovers. At 23b he says going after the second prize on behalf of nous requires a different device, like having other arrows than the previous arguments.
limits: >
  Does not establish that the entire dialogue is structured as a contest. The metaphor surfaces at this transitional moment; whether it recurs later is not addressed here.
review_status: accepted
```

english_gloss: ""

feature_label: classification_by_collection_into_one_genus
observation: "At 25c, Socrates instructs Protarchus to add the pairs 'drier and wetter,' 'more and less,' 'quicker and slower,' 'greater and smaller' to those items earlier collected into the single nature that receives the more-and-less. When Protarchus identifies this nature as 'that of the unlimited' at 25d, Socrates agrees and then directs him to collect together the 'offspring of the limit' (ten tou peratos gennan) in the same way — by gathering limit-like items such as 'the equal and the double' into one genus."
textual_basis: "Socrates uses the method of collecting many instances into one unified kind (eis hen phuseos, 25c) for the unlimited, then explicitly calls for the same procedure for the limit-like class (25d: 'just as we collected the unlimited into one, so too we must collect the limit-like'), naming 'the equal and the double' as examples (ten tou isou kai diplasiou, 25d)."
limits: "This observation records the explicit classificatory method of collection into a single genus for the unlimited and limit-like kinds; it does not assess whether the collection is complete or whether the division into limit/unlimited exhausts the possible ontological categories."
review_status: accepted
```

textual_basis: "Socrates explicitly directs the respondent's participation at 25c (sunakoloutheson toi logoi), at 25c (prosthes de, listing pairs to add), and at 25d (summeignu de ge eis auten... ten au tou peratos gennan). limits: "This observation notes the procedural control Socrates exercises over the respondent's participation in the classificatory work; it does not interpret these instructions as dialectical manipulation or claim that Protarchus is being misled."

textual_basis: "Protarchus says he thinks he understands the first two, but regarding the third he states he does not quite grasp what Socrates means (26c)."
limits: "This observation records the qualified assent pattern; it does not interpret why Protarchus is confused or assess the difficulty of the concept."
review_status: accepted
```

feature_label: divine_assistance_invoked
observation: "When Protarchus asks Socrates to explain what form the mixed kind has, Socrates defers to a god, saying a god would reveal it if one of the gods becomes hearkening to his prayers; he then reports that a god has just now become a friend to them (25b)."
textual_basis: "Socrates says at 25b: theos men oun, anper ge emais euchais epekoos gignetai tis theon — a god, that is, if some god becomes hearkening to my prayers. Protarchus tells him to pray and consider. Socrates then says: kai moi dokei tis, o Protarche, auton philos hemin nunde gegonenai — and it seems to me, Protarchus, that some god has just now become a friend to us. This marks a turn away from dialectical classification toward divine disclosure."
limits: "This records the invocation of divine assistance at the juncture where the third kind is introduced. It does not claim that the subsequent account is mythic rather than dialectical, only that the text marks a transition with divine language."
review_status: accepted
```

observation: Socrates classifies the victorious mixed life as part of the third genus — the mixture of limit and unlimited — and refines the description: the mixed life is not mixed from merely two things but from all the unlimited things bound by limit.
textual_basis: At 27d, Socrates recalls that the mixed life of pleasure and wisdom was established as victor. He then asks what genus it belongs to, and answers that it is part of the third genus. He clarifies that this mixture is not from two things but from all the unlimited things bound by limit, so the victorious life is part of that genus.
limits: This observation records the classification of the mixed life into the genus framework; it does not evaluate whether the classification is consistent with earlier passages or whether the four-genus scheme is fully applied here.

```

textual_basis: Philebus asks Protarchus whether he has chosen to speak on Philebus's behalf (28b), confirming the substitution. textual_basis: Socrates states at 28c that he was truly dignifying in play when he asked about the kind to which intellect and knowledge belong, explicitly framing his earlier questioning as playful and echoing Philebus's earlier remark that Socrates was dignifying his own god (28b).

feature_id: feature_candidate_2397
feature_label: categories_enumerated_in_sequence
observation: At 27a-27b Socrates enumerates four kinds (gene) in a numbered sequence: first the unlimited (apeiron), second the limit (peras), third the mixed and generated substance (meikte kai gegenemene ousia), and fourth the cause of mixture and generation (tes meixeos aitia kai geneseos).
textual_basis: After distinguishing the maker (to poioun) from what is made (to poioumenon) and what comes to be (to gignomenon) at 27a, Socrates introduces a fourth kind, the cause, at 27b. He then offers an explicit sequential enumeration of all four kinds, framing each with ordinal language: first (proton), second (deuteron), third (triton), fourth (tetarten).
limits: The observation notes only the presence of an ordered enumeration of four kinds within this span. It does not assess whether earlier passages in the Philebus establish these same four kinds or whether the four-fold division is stable throughout the dialogue.
review_status: accepted
```

feature_label: cause_rules_what_serves_generation
observation: At 27a, Socrates distinguishes cause (aitia) from what is enslaved to cause for the sake of generation (to douleuon eis genesin aitia), characterizing their relation through a master-servant metaphor.
textual_basis: Socrates asks whether cause and that which is subservient to cause for generation are different and not the same (allo ara kai ou tauton aitia t'esti kai to douleuon eis genesin aitia, 27a). The verb douleuon (being a slave or servant to) applies a social relation of subordination to the metaphysical relation between cause and what it produces.
limits: The observation notes the metaphorical register of douleuon but does not claim that Socrates is deploying a full craft analogy here, only that a master-servant relation structures the distinction between cause and generated result.
review_status: accepted
```

  Protarchus rejects the chance-governance view on grounds that it is "not even
  holy," and affirms the nous-governance view as "worthy" of the visible
  cosmic order, thereby introducing a piety criterion into the argument.
textual_basis: >-
  At 28e Protarchus calls the irrational-chance alternative "not even holy"
  and says affirming nous as ordering all things is "worthy" of the sight of
  the cosmos, sun, moon, stars, and all revolution. The argument shifts from
  rational to religious-pious grounds.
limits: >-
  The observation records the presence of a piety criterion, not that Socrates
  endorses it or that the argument depends on it. It does not assert that this
  is a myth per se, only that piety language demarcates the acceptable from the

feature_label: microcosm_dependent_on_macrocosm
observation: "Socrates asserts a one-way dependency: the cosmic elements (fire, earth, etc.) nourish, generate, and increase the corresponding elements in living beings, not the reverse. Protarchus treats the direction of dependency as so obvious that the question does not deserve an answer (29c-29d)."
textual_basis: "At 29c Socrates contrasts the small, weak fire 'in us' with the cosmic fire marvelous in quantity, beauty, and power, then asks whether the cosmic fire is nourished by the fire in us or vice versa. Protarchus replies that the question is not worth answering. Socrates then extends the same structure to earth and the other elements discussed earlier (29d)."
limits: "This observation records the structure of the dependency claim, not whether it is independently defended or whether the argument presupposes unstated cosmological commitments."
review_status: accepted
```

observation: "Socrates posits a physiological condition under which pleasure and pain arise: the dissolution of harmony in living beings produces a simultaneous dissolution of nature and generation of pains, while the restoration of harmony and return to its own nature produces pleasure. He frames this with a remark about speaking briefly about the greatest matters."
textual_basis: "At 31d Socrates states that when harmony is dissolved in living beings, there occurs at the same time a dissolution of nature and a generation of pains. Conversely, when harmony is restored and returns to its own nature, pleasure is generated. The formula about speaking briefly about the greatest matters (31d) marks the compressed, hypothesis-like character of the claim."
limits: "This observation notes the conditional structure of the claim, not whether the physiological model is empirically correct or how it relates to other Platonic accounts of pleasure."
review_status: accepted
```

textual_basis: At 29a Socrates uses hortatory subjunctives (let us agree, let us share the risk and participate in the blame) to frame the inquiry as a joint undertaking where both participants bear the consequences of the argument. This follows the existing pattern of procedural_agreement_to_inquiry.
limits: This observation does not establish what the four elements are ontologically; it only records that Socrates frames the next phase of the argument as a shared risk.

The new kind is characterized as belonging to the soul alone apart from the body, arising through anticipation (prosdokēma)."
textual_basis: "At 32b Socrates says 'let us posit this then as one kind (hen eidos) of pain and pleasure in these affections,' treating the body-based pair as the first kind. At 32c he then introduces the soul-based anticipatory pair as 'another kind of pleasure and pain' (hēdonēs kai lypēs heteron eidos), explicitly marking it as the second in a numbered sequence."
limits: "This observation records the explicit numbering of kinds in the text; it does not claim that this is an exhaustive taxonomy or that the two kinds together constitute a complete classification of pleasure and pain."
review_status: accepted
```

textual_basis: "At 32b Socrates recapitulates the body-based kind: destruction is pain, restoration to one's own nature is pleasure. At 32c Protarchus confirms the new kind as 'another kind of pleasure and pain, the one arising from the soul itself apart from the body through anticipation' (to chōris tou sōmatos autēs tēs psychēs dia prosdokias gignomenon). Socrates affirms this distinction."
limits: "This observation records the body/soul contrast as an explicit textual distinction. It does not claim that this is the first appearance of the contrast in Plato, nor does it assess whether the distinction is stable throughout the dialogue."
review_status: accepted
```

observation: "Protarchus dismisses Socrates' question about the direction of nourishment between the human body and the cosmic body as not worth asking, treating the answer as too obvious to require explicit statement."
textual_basis: "At 29e-30a Socrates asks whether the human body is nourished from the cosmic body or vice versa; Protarchus replies that this too is not worth asking (29e), refusing to give a direct answer and indicating the answer is self-evident."
limits: "This records the turn-level phenomenon of respondent minimal engagement. It does not analyze whether Protarchus is correct that the answer is obvious."

```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_philebus_0134
source_work: Philebus
stephanus_span: 31e-32a
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/philebus.txt
  stephanus_span: 31e-32a
  start_marker: 31e
  end_marker: 32a
  start_char: 41141
  end_char: 41879
  text_sha256: 8dda929cba3a8fc5b795d84b72fb593c5a65b7c635d6e8529dd5c521401b08fd
greek_terms:
  - πείνη
  - λύσις
  - λύπη
  - ἐδωδή
  - πλήρωσις
  - ἡδονή
  - δίψος
  - φθορά
  - παρά φύσιν
  - κατὰ φύσιν
english_gloss: ""
feature_family: definition_ladder
feature_id: feature_candidate_2541
feature_label: definition_by_paired_opposites
observation: |
  Socrates defines pleasure and pain for bodily processes by pairing each with an opposite process, each organized by the nature/against-nature distinction. Hunger (peine) is a dissolution and pain; eating (edode) is a replenishment and pleasure. Thirst (dipsos) is a destruction and pain; the power that replenishes the dried-out with moisture is pleasure. Abnormal separation and dissolution from stifling heat is pain; natural restoration and cooling is pleasure. Shivering is the unnatural coagulation of the creature's moisture producing pain; the natural return to the same state is pleasure.
textual_basis: |
  At 31e-32a Socrates defines hunger as "dissolution and pain" (lysis kai lupe), eating as "replenishment and pleasure" (plerosis...hedone), and thirst similarly. Each condition is explicitly marked as either para phusin (against nature) or kata phusin (according to nature). The pattern is explicit: the unnatural state produces pain, the natural restoration produces pleasure.
limits: |
  This observation covers only the bodily pleasures and pains defined in 31e-32a, not the psychic pleasures treated elsewhere in the dialogue. The observation does not claim that these are exhaustive definitions; Socrates himself hedges with "see if the account is moderate" (ei soi metrios ho logos) at 32a.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_philebus_0135

  - κατὰ φύσιν
  - τοῦ ζῴου
english_gloss: ""
feature_family: definition_ladder
feature_id: feature_candidate_2631
feature_label: criterion_proposed
observation: |
  Socrates uses the distinction between what is "according to nature" (kata phusin) and "against nature" (para phusin) as a criterion for classifying bodily conditions as either pleasure or pain. The natural restoration or return to a prior state is pleasure; the unnatural dissolution or coagulation is pain.
textual_basis: |
  In the contrast pairs at 31e-32a, each condition is explicitly labeled with either para phusin (the dissolution from heat, the coagulation from cold) or kata phusin (the restoration, cooling, return). The distinction functions as the organizing criterion for the entire set of definitions.
limits: |
  This is a local criterion for bodily pleasures and pains in this passage. It is not claimed as a universal criterion for all pleasure and pain, nor does this passage apply it to psychic pleasures. The observation notes the presence of the criterion, not its adequacy.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_philebus_0136
source_work: Philebus
stephanus_span: 31e-32a

  source_path: raw/plato/greek/philebus.txt
  stephanus_span: 31e-32a
  start_marker: 31e
  end_marker: 32a
  start_char: 41141
  end_char: 41879
  text_sha256: 8dda929cba3a8fc5b795d84b72fb593c5a65b7c635d6e8529dd5c521401b08fd
greek_terms:
  - ἑνὶ λόγῳ σκόπει εἴ σοι μέτριος ὁ λόγος
english_gloss: ""
feature_family: turn_geometry
feature_id: feature_candidate_720
feature_label: explicit_procedure_control
observation: |
  After offering a series of definitions of bodily pleasure and pain through nature/against-nature pairs, Socrates explicitly presents the entire account as a hypothesis to be tested: "in one account, see if the account is moderate for you" (heni logoi skopei ei soi metrios ho logos). He treats the definitional framework as a provisional claim to be evaluated by the respondent.
textual_basis: |
  At 32a Socrates says "heni logoi skopei ei soi metrios ho logos" — he marks the account just given (the nature/against-nature classification of pleasures and pains) as something for Protarchus to examine and judge whether it is metrios (moderate, fitting). This is a textbook example of presenting the result of an inquiry as a hypothesis condition for the respondent to test.
limits: |
  The observation records the hypothesis-testing framing but does not evaluate whether the account actually is moderate or whether Protarchus's subsequent assent constitutes adequate testing. The observation only covers the explicit marking of the account as a hypothesis to be examined.
review_status: accepted
```

This recapitulation is hedged with a conditional about whether it will serve the argument."
textual_basis: "At 33c Socrates says they will examine the point again if it bears on the argument, and then immediately proposes to take up memory (mnemen) first, and possibly perception (aisthesin) even before memory. The meta-level planning and conditional hedge mark a deliberate turn-geometry move."
limits: "This observation records the recapitulation turn structure, not the content of memory or perception doctrine. It does not claim that the postponement implies any doctrinal commitment."

```

The classification is presented as a posited framework (thes... keistho)."
textual_basis: "At 33d Socrates says 'thes' (posit) and distinguishes two kinds of bodily pathemata, then asks for Protarchus's acceptance ('keistho'). The framework is explicitly hypothetical, presented for agreement before being used in argument."
limits: "This observation records the hypothetical framing turn; it does not analyze the philosophical adequacy of the somatic/psychic distinction drawn."

textual_basis: "At 33d Socrates frames the distinction as 'ta men de me di' amphoin ionta... lanthanein, ta de di' amphoin me lanthanein' — a clean dichotomous contrast between unnoticed and noticed affections, keyed to whether they traverse both body and soul."

```yaml
observation_id: obs_philebus_0142

greek_terms: []
english_gloss: ""
feature_family: elenchus
feature_id: feature_candidate_2687
feature_label: third_state_deduction
observation: Socrates at 32e deduces a third disposition (tritē diathesis) of living beings, neither pleasure nor pain, from the premises that pleasure arises in restoration and pain in destruction, concluding that when neither process occurs, a neutral state necessarily follows.
textual_basis: Socrates sets out the premises at 32e: if what is said is truly so, that pain belongs to processes of being destroyed (diaphtheiromenon) and pleasure to processes of being restored (anasozomenon), then one must consider those beings undergoing neither destruction nor restoration; he then asks Protarchus whether it is not wholly necessary that every living being at that time feel neither pain nor pleasure, great or small, and Protarchus assents (ananke men oun). Socrates then names this a third disposition (tritē...diathesis).
limits: This observation records the deduction and its premises as stated in the text; it does not evaluate whether the deduction is valid or whether the third state is later identified with a specific kind of life.
review_status: accepted
```

observation: >
  Socrates defines recollection (anamnesis) as distinct from memory (mneme),
  providing two conditions: (1) when the soul, without the body, recovers in
  itself what it once experienced together with the body; (2) when the soul,
  having lost memory of either a perception or a piece of learning, recovers
  it again in itself (34b-34c).
textual_basis: >
  At 34b Socrates asks whether recollection differs from memory, then at
  34b-c provides a two-part definition introduced by "whenever..." (hotan...)
  followed by Protarchus's strong assent at both steps.
limits: >
  This observation records the definition offered; it does not assess whether
  the definition is adequate or how it relates to the recollection doctrine
  in other dialogues.

feature_id: feature_candidate_383
feature_label: conclusion_scope_limited
observation: >
  Socrates announces that the argument (ho logos) has revealed what has befallen them
  from their discussion (35c). He then frames the conclusion as what "this argument
  tells us" (houtos ho logos phesin), attributing the thesis to the argument rather
  than asserting it in his own voice, while also noting that the argument seems to
  want to reveal a certain form of life (35d).
textual_basis: >
  At 35c Socrates says they understand what has resulted "from these arguments."
  At 35d he twice uses "the argument" (ho logos) as the subject that reveals,
  demonstrates, and chooses. He closes by saying the argument seems (phainetai)
  to want to reveal a form of life (biou eidos), hedging the conclusion with
  an appearance verb.
limits: >
  Does not assess whether the argument is sound; notes only the attribution of
  force to the logos itself and the tentative framing of its direction.
review_status: accepted
```

  "a form of life" (biou eidos ti). This marks the transition from

textual_basis: >
  At 35d Socrates says "eti de kai tode peri tauta tauta katanoesomen,"
  explicitly marking a continuation of the same inquiry. He then states
  that the argument appears to want to reveal a biou eidos (form or kind
  of life), setting up the next topic for examination.

textual_basis: At 35e-36a Socrates asks what happens when someone is in the middle (en mesōi) of replenishment and depletion (35e). He then describes a case: the person feels pain due to the pathos, remembers pleasures whose occurrence would stop the pain, but is not yet filled (35e-36a). Protarchus assents that the person is in the middle of the affections (36a).
limits: The observation records the introduction of an intermediate term in a classificatory schema; it does not assess whether the intermediate is a third genus in the dialogue's overall taxonomy of pleasure and pain.
review_status: accepted
```

feature_label: definition_by_two_criteria
observation: When Socrates asks whether the person in the intermediate state is in pain or pleasure, Protarchus offers a definition by two criteria: the person suffers a double pain (diplēi tini lupēi), one according to the body in the pathos and another according to the soul in a longing of expectation.
textual_basis: At 36a, Socrates asks whether the person in the middle is pained or pleased. Protarchus replies that the person is pained with a double pain — kata men to sōma in the pathos, kata de tēn psuchēn in a longing of expectation (prosdokias tini pothōi). This distinguishes the bodily and psychic components of the intermediate state.
limits: This observation records the two-criteria structure of the reply; it does not evaluate whether this double pain counts as a distinct class in the pleasure-pain taxonomy or whether the soul's contribution is genuinely independent of the body's.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml

greek_terms: ["μεμνῆται δὲ τῶν ἡδέων"]
english_gloss: ""
feature_family: turn_geometry
feature_id: feature_candidate_1090
feature_label: hypothetical_prompt_or_scenario
observation: Socrates constructs the intermediate-state analysis by conjuring a hypothetical scenario: a person who is in pain, remembers pleasures, and is not yet filled. The scenario is framed as a question about what "we" should say ("phōmen ē mē phōmen") about such a person.
textual_basis: At 35e-36a Socrates poses a hypothetical case — "hotan en mesōi toutōn gignētai" (when one is in the middle of these) — and unpacks it through a series of conditions (pain from the pathos, memory of absent pleasures, not yet filled). He then asks Protarchus "phōmen ē mē phōmen" (shall we say or not say) that the person is in the middle of the affections (36a).
limits: This observation records the hypothetical-prompt structure of the exchange; it does not claim that every hypothetical scenario in the Philebus functions identically, nor does it evaluate whether the hypothesis is later accepted or modified.
review_status: accepted
```

observation: |
  Socrates explicitly recalls the earlier comparison of lives from the dialogue, repeating the claim that the one who chooses the life of intelligence and thought needs neither great nor small pleasure (33a-33b). Protarchus confirms the earlier formulation, and Socrates builds on this recapitulation to derive a further claim about the life's divine character.
textual_basis: |
  At 33b Socrates uses the phrase "it was said at that time in the comparison of lives" (errethē gar pou tote en tē parabolē tōn biōn), directly citing the earlier argument and having Protarchus confirm the restatement with "indeed it was said so" (kai mala houtōs errethē).
limits: |
  This observation notes the recapitulation structure only; it does not evaluate whether the recalled claim is identical in wording to the earlier passage or whether the recapitulation is accurate.
review_status: accepted
```

english_gloss: ""
feature_family: turn_geometry
feature_id: feature_candidate_1129
feature_label: terminological_control
observation: Socrates instructs Protarchus to "change the names only," renaming what was called forgetfulness (lethe) of the soul as insensibility (anaisthesia), on the grounds that forgetfulness properly involves loss of memory and memory has not yet been established in the discussion.
textual_basis: At 33e Socrates argues it is absurd to speak of a loss (apobole) of something that neither is nor has come to be; since memory (mneme) has not yet been discussed, the term lethe is premature. He then says "change the names only" and substitutes anaisthesia for lethe when the soul is unaffected by bodily disturbances.
limits: This observation records a terminological control move within a single argumentative sequence; it does not assess whether the renaming is justified or whether Socrates consistently uses these terms elsewhere in the Philebus.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_philebus_0158
source_work: Philebus
stephanus_span: 37c-37d
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/philebus.txt
  stephanus_span: 37c-37d
  start_marker: 37c
  end_marker: 37d
  start_char: 52623
  end_char: 53405
  text_sha256: d0dcf77fb0789cfdaeb9c67dd8745054d427f3a0185288c8e4fd00c0719b11f6
greek_terms: ["ποιά τις", "ποί' ἄττα", "ποιώ τινε"]
english_gloss: ""
feature_family: definition_ladder
feature_id: feature_candidate_2721
feature_label: qualities_predicated_of_pleasure_and_pain
observation: "Socrates draws Protarchus into agreeing that pleasure and pain are not merely what they are (haper esti) but also become qualified (poia tina). The contrast between being something and being qualified in some way is explicitly posed as a point requiring agreement (dihomologêteon) at 37c."
textual_basis: "At 37c Socrates proposes the question of whether opinion (doxa) and each of the pair (hekatera) — referring back to pleasure and pain — become not only what they are but also 'of a certain quality' (poia tis). He then extends the point explicitly: is it the case that pleasure and pain are only what they are (haper esti) and do not become qualified (poiô tine), and this must be agreed (dihomologêteon). Protarchus assents. At 37d, Socrates applies the quality 'ponêria' (badness) to both opinion and pleasure, then 'orthotês' (correctness), treating both as qualities that can accrue (prosgignêtai) to them."
limits: "This observation records the structure of the argument that pleasure and pain admit of qualitative predicates. It does not assess whether this commits the dialogue to a substance-quality distinction in the metaphysical sense, nor does it evaluate the validity of treating pleasure as qualified by moral predicates."
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_philebus_0159
source_work: Philebus
stephanus_span: 37c-37d
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/philebus.txt
  stephanus_span: 37c-37d
  start_marker: 37c
  end_marker: 37d
  start_char: 52623
  end_char: 53405
  text_sha256: d0dcf77fb0789cfdaeb9c67dd8745054d427f3a0185288c8e4fd00c0719b11f6
greek_terms: ["πονηρία", "προσγίγνηται", "ὀρθότης", "ὀρθὴν"]
english_gloss: ""
feature_family: elenchus
feature_id: feature_candidate_2722
feature_label: moral_quality_accrues_to_pleasure_as_to_opinion
observation: "Socrates secures Protarchus's agreement that if ponêria (badness) accrues (prosgignêtai) to something, then opinion becomes bad (ponêran ... doxan) and pleasure likewise becomes bad (ponêran ... hêdonên). He then immediately applies the same structure to orthotês (correctness), yielding orthê doxa and, by the same token (tauton), orthê hêdonê."
textual_basis: "At 37d Socrates asks: if ponêria accrues to these things, shall we say that opinion becomes bad, and likewise pleasure bad? Protarchus assents emphatically (alla ti mên). Socrates then immediately asks: what if orthotês or its opposite accrues to them? Shall we not say that opinion is orthê if it possesses orthotês, and the same for pleasure? Protarchus answers 'necessary' (anangkaion). The structural parallelism between opinion and pleasure with respect to moral-epistemic qualities is the explicit content of this exchange."
limits: "This observation records the argumentative move in which pleasure is treated as capable of bearing moral-epistemic qualities (bad, correct) in the same way opinion does. It does not claim that Socrates endorses this view or that the dialogue ultimately accepts it; the record only notes that Protarchus assents under elenctic pressure."
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_philebus_0160
source_work: Philebus
stephanus_span: 37c-37d
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/philebus.txt
  stephanus_span: 37c-37d
  start_marker: 37c
  end_marker: 37d
  start_char: 52623
  end_char: 53405
  text_sha256: d0dcf77fb0789cfdaeb9c67dd8745054d427f3a0185288c8e4fd00c0719b11f6
greek_terms: ["μεγάλαι", "σμικραί", "σφόδρα"]
english_gloss: ""
feature_family: turn_geometry
feature_id: feature_candidate_2723
feature_label: appeal_to_prior_agreement_as_shortcut
observation: "When Protarchus agrees that the question of whether pleasure and pain are merely what they are or also qualified must be settled, Socrates immediately dismisses the difficulty of the question by appealing to an earlier agreement: they already said (palai gar eipomen) that pleasures and pains are large, small, and intense (megalai, smikrai, sphodra). Protarchus's one-word assent (pantapasi men oun) closes the sub-move without fresh argument."
textual_basis: "At 37c Socrates says 'it is not difficult to see that they are also qualified, for we said long ago that they become large, small, and intense — both pains and pleasures.' Protarchus responds at 37d with 'absolutely' (pantapasi men oun). Socrates uses this quick retrospective appeal to bypass any resistance Protarchus might have had to treating pleasure as qualified, moving directly to the moral-quality question."
limits: "This observation records the procedural move of citing a prior agreement to close a question rapidly. It does not evaluate whether the earlier agreement (from an earlier part of the dialogue) genuinely established the point or whether Protarchus's assent is merely acquiescent."
review_status: accepted
```

observation: "Socrates distinguishes false pleasures from bad (ponēras) pleasures, deferring discussion of the latter ('oligon hysteron') and proposing to examine false pleasures separately ('kat' allon tropon'). Protarchus tentatively accepts the inquiry conditional on their existence."
textual_basis: "At 41a Protarchus grants that pleasures might be called bad (ponēras) on some other ground than falsity; Socrates at 41a-b marks the distinction explicitly: the bad pleasures will be discussed later, while the false ones must now be examined (lekton). This sets up the next elenctic strand."
limits: "Does not establish that false pleasures exist; Protarchus accepts the inquiry only conditionally (eiper ge eisin). Does not evaluate whether the distinction between false and bad pleasures holds up later."
review_status: accepted
```

feature_label: definition_by_criterion
observation: Socrates argues that false pleasures can arise in the same way as false opinions. At 40d he states these mechanisms are what produce false opinion and false believing, then at 40e he extends the claim to pleasures: they are wicked only insofar as they are false.
textual_basis: The segment opens with Socrates identifying the mechanisms that produce false opinion and false believing (40d). Then at 40e he concludes that pleasures are wicked solely by being false. This sets up a definitional criterion: a pleasure's moral quality is determined exclusively by its truth status.
limits: The observation does not evaluate whether the argument succeeds or whether false pleasures are a coherent category. It only records that Socrates offers truth/falsity as the sole criterion for classifying pleasures as wicked.
review_status: accepted
```

observation: Socrates traces the generation of opinion (doxa) and the attempt at forming a judgment to memory and perception at 38b-38c, and links true and false doxa to the earlier claim that pleasure and pain follow true and false doxa.
textual_basis: At 38b, Socrates recapitulates that doxa can be true or false and that pleasure and pain often follow true and false doxa. He then asks at 38b-c whether doxa and forming a judgment arise each time from memory and perception, and Protarchus strongly agrees.
limits: This observation records the stated genealogy of doxa in this passage. It does not evaluate whether this genealogy is consistent with other dialogues or whether it is subsequently revised.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_philebus_0172
temporary: true
source_work: Philebus

  end_marker: 42a
  start_char: 61364
  end_char: 62110
  text_sha256: acb248f684f4cbf09d988b53c8264bee1f6af9f5be237e457793658adada3c39
greek_terms: []
english_gloss: ""
feature_family: elenchus
feature_id: feature_candidate_2747
feature_label: analogy_to_perception
observation: |
  Socrates introduces a sensory-perception analogy for the judgment of pleasures and pains: just as viewing magnitudes from afar or near (to porrōthen kai enguthen horan) destroys truth and produces false opinion (42a), so the same effect occurs with pleasures and pains. Protarchus accepts this claim and even intensifies it (polu men oun mallon).
textual_basis: |
  At 41e-42a Socrates asks whether, just as in sight (en men opsei) near and distant viewing falsifies judgments about size (ta megethē), the same thing happens with pleasures and pains. Protarchus answers affirmatively and emphatically. Socrates then distinguishes this moment from the earlier argument: previously (41e), false and true opinions were said to fill pleasures and pains with their own condition (pathēmatos); now the analogy shifts to a spatial-perceptual model of distortion.
limits: |
  This observation records the analogy's introduction and Protarchus's assent, not whether the analogy is later qualified or whether it ultimately succeeds. It does not assess the truth of the perceptual model.
review_status: accepted
```

greek_terms: []
english_gloss: ""
feature_family: turn_geometry
feature_id: feature_candidate_1122
feature_label: argument_transition_marker
observation: |
  Socrates marks a sharp turn in the argument at 42a: the present point (to nun) is opposite (enantion) to what was said a little earlier (tōi smikron emprosthe). When Protarchus asks what he means, Socrates recapitulates the earlier position: that false and true opinions filled pleasures and pains with their own affective condition (pathēmatos).
textual_basis: |
  Socrates states at 42a: "Opposite indeed has the present become to what a little before" (enantion dē to nun tōi smikron emprosthe gegonen). He then clarifies that previously (tote men), false and true opinions simultaneously filled (anepimplasan) pleasures and pains with their own condition. This explicit contrast marks an argumentative pivot from the opinion-mediated account to the new perceptual-distortion model.
limits: |
  This records the transitional structure at this juncture, not the content of either the earlier or the new argument. The observation does not claim that Socrates is correcting himself or that either account is privileged.
review_status: accepted
```

textual_basis: "Protarchus responds with emphatic agreement (35a), a rhetorical question conceding the point, assent that the desirer desires something, a confirming 'yes', a strong 'necessarily', and a further 'yes' across 35a-35b, each assent advancing the chain of reasoning that culminates in attributing the capacity to contact filling to the soul."

observation: "Socrates argues at 35a-35b that the soul, not the body, contacts the object of desire (filling) because the body experiences only emptying and cannot reach toward what it has never experienced."
textual_basis: "Socrates asks whether someone emptied for the first time could contact filling by sensation or memory (35a), establishes that the desirer does not desire what they are experiencing (35b), rules out the body because it is being emptied (35b), and concludes the soul must be what contacts the filling (35b)."
limits: "This is an argument about the psychology of desire, not a general theory of soul-body relations. The conclusion is provisional within the dialectical context of the Philebus discussion of pleasure and desire."
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_philebus_0176
source_work: Philebus
stephanus_span: 42b-42c
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/philebus.txt
  stephanus_span: 42b-42c

  end_char: 62964
  text_sha256: b95856485a58094521d36f3608243847c116c4abb38cae0b14cbd978b2baec15
greek_terms:
  - φαινόμενον
  - ὄν
english_gloss: ""
feature_family: forms_trajectory
feature_id: feature_candidate_1231
feature_label: appearance_vs_reality_construal
observation: |
  Socrates argues that pleasures and pains can appear larger or smaller than they are depending on whether they are viewed from near or far, or set alongside their opposites. He then introduces a methodological cut: one must separate what merely appears (to phainomenon) from what is (on), and should not call the apparent pleasure or pain "correct" or "true" if it does not correspond to what is real.
textual_basis: |
  At 42b Socrates states that pleasures and pains appear greater and more intense when placed alongside their opposites (para to lypēron... para hēdonas). At 42b-42c he says one must cut away (apotemomenos) the appearing-but-not-being (to phainomenon all' ouk on) from each, and that one should not call this apparent portion "correct" (orthōs phainomenon) or "true" (alēthes).
limits: |
  This observation records the appearance/reality contrast applied to hedonic states. It does not assess whether the argument succeeds, nor does it claim that this passage invokes the theory of Forms. The passage operates at the level of perceptual and comparative judgments about pleasures and pains.
review_status: accepted
---
```
