```yaml
observation: "Protarchus resists Socrates' claim that pleasure can be false (38a). He concedes that opinion can be false, but denies that pleasure itself can be called false, thus blocking an elenctic extension of the attribute from opinion to pleasure."
textual_basis: "At 37e Socrates argues that pleasure often occurs with false opinion, implying pleasure may share in falsehood. At 38a Protarchus accepts false opinion but rejects false pleasure: no one would call pleasure itself false."
limits: "The observation records only the denial; it does not evaluate whether Protarchus' resistance is philosophically cogent or merely verbal."
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
feature_id: feature_candidate_1246
feature_label: demiourgos_production_mapping
observation: Socrates describes two internal craftsmen (demiourgoi) operating in the soul: a scribe (grammateus) who writes logoi from the convergence of memory and perception, and a painter (zographos) who subsequently creates images in the soul from what has been opined and said.
textual_basis: At 39a Socrates states that memory converging with perceptions writes logoi in our souls, and when this scribe (grammateus) writes truly, true opinion and true logoi result. At 39b he introduces another craftsman (heteron demiourgon), a painter (zographon), who after the scribe writes images (eikonas) in the soul.
limits: This observation records the two-stage craftsman model as a textual feature; it does not assess whether the model is coherent or whether it accurately describes cognition.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
greek_terms:
  - ἀληθεῖς
  - ψευδεῖς
  - ἡδοναὶ
  - λύπαι

  - προσδοκίαι
  - δόξαι
english_gloss: ""
feature_family: definition_ladder
feature_id: feature_candidate_1573
feature_label: deficiendum_marked_for_inquiry
observation: |
  At 36c Socrates proposes that the examination of pleasures and pains (the pathêmata under discussion) proceed by asking whether each is true or false — or some true and others not. Protarchus immediately challenges the very intelligibility of the question, asking how pleasures or pains could possibly be false. Socrates responds by drawing an analogy to fears, expectations, and opinions, all of which can be true or false.
textual_basis: |
  Socrates asks at 36c whether the pleasures and pains under discussion should be called true or false or some true and others not. Protarchus asks how pleasures or pains could possibly be false. Socrates counters by asking whether fears, expectations, and opinions can be true or false — implying that if doxai can be true or false, the question about pleasures and pains is intelligible.
limits: |
  This observation records the introduction of the truth-falsehood criterion for pleasures and pains and Protarchus's initial challenge. It does not assess whether the analogy to doxai succeeds or whether pleasures can in fact be false.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_philebus_0183
source_work: Philebus
stephanus_span: 36b-36c

  text_sha256: 5fb8d425743bd653a88c7c6765038f1edec5b577cf33e302f27c624d5fcf3d7a
greek_terms:
  - κενούμενος
  - πληρώσεως
  - ἀνελπίστως
  - διπλοῦν
english_gloss: ""
feature_family: elenchus
feature_id: feature_candidate_380
feature_label: forced_alternative
observation: |
  At 36b-36c Socrates presses Protarchus through a dense series of short question-and-answer steps about mixed states of pleasure and pain, specifically the case of someone being emptied while hopeful of replenishment (36b) and the opposite case of someone being emptied without hope of replenishment (36c). In the latter case Socrates identifies a "double" pathos of pain where Protarchus had previously assumed a simple pain condition. Protarchus assents at every step.
textual_basis: |
  At 36b Socrates asks whether one who hopes for replenishment (while remembering it) rejoices while simultaneously being in pain from the emptying. Protarchus says "necessity" (anankê). Socrates then establishes that a human being and other animals can be pained and pleased simultaneously. At 36c Socrates describes the hopeless case as producing a "double" condition concerning pains (to diploun... pathos), which Protarchus had previously thought was simply double — and Protarchus agrees emphatically (alêthestata).
limits: |
  This observation notes the forced-assent pattern that refines the mixed-state analysis. It does not evaluate whether the psychological model is correct or exhaustive.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
  text_sha256: d013ef879821fbb4cb7fef544766cd4cd7fc576efc230f62e7308977f42d4974
greek_terms: []
english_gloss: ""
feature_family: elenchus
feature_id: feature_candidate_071
feature_label: assent_chain
observation: Socrates leads Protarchus through a syllogistic inference concluding that pleasures and pains can be simultaneously present with simultaneous perceptions of these contrary states.
textual_basis: At 41c Socrates asks Protarchus to syllogize what follows in these cases, and Protarchus assents. Socrates then states the conclusion at 41d: when these conditions obtain, pleasures and pains lie alongside each other with simultaneous perceptions of contrary states, which Protarchus affirms.
limits: This observation identifies the assent-chain structure but does not evaluate whether the inference is valid or whether pleasure and pain are in fact simultaneous in the relevant sense.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_philebus_0191

greek_terms: []
english_gloss: ""
feature_family: methodological_distinction
feature_id: feature_candidate_2764
feature_label: extreme_case_method_for_inquiry_target
observation: Socrates proposes that to see the greatest pleasures one must look not at health but at sickness (45c). He frames this as a methodological principle for investigating the nature of pleasure, specifically its magnitude and intensity.
textual_basis: At 45c Socrates states that if someone wants to see the greatest pleasures, they should observe those who go into sickness, not health. He clarifies this is about seeking the megethos (magnitude) of pleasure and where intensity (to sphodra) occurs, not about whether the sick feel more pleasures.
limits: This observation identifies the methodological move without evaluating whether Socrates consistently applies it elsewhere or whether the method is philosophically sound.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
feature_id: feature_candidate_2765
feature_label: scope_delimitation
observation: At 45c-45d Socrates explicitly delimits the inquiry target: he is seeking the magnitude (megethos) and intensity (sphodra) of pleasure, not the quantity (plethos) of pleasures. He repeats the distinction at 45d when asking about greater pleasures in hubris "not in number but in intensity and degree."
textual_basis: At 45c Socrates says not to think he is asking whether the severely ill enjoy more pleasures than the healthy, but rather that he is seeking megethos of pleasure and where sphodra occurs. At 45d he reiterates the distinction: "not in number, but those exceeding in intensity (sphodra) and degree (mallon)."
limits: This observation records the scope delimitation made by Socrates without evaluating whether the distinction between magnitude and number of pleasures is successfully maintained throughout the dialogue.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_philebus_0193
source_work: Philebus
stephanus_span: 43e-44a
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/philebus.txt
  stephanus_span: 43e-44a
  start_marker: 43e
  end_marker: 44a
  start_char: 65447
  end_char: 66292
  text_sha256: 40b392b2b7fb58e708aaa18df47d667203eba6f8168402bd6eec25d6d3c94fa4
greek_terms: []
english_gloss: ''
feature_family: elenchus
feature_id: feature_candidate_221
feature_label: false_dichotomy_rejected
observation: Socrates uses a threefold classification (gold, silver, neither) as an analogy to reject the claim that the intermediate life (neither pleasant nor painful) can correctly be called pleasant or painful; he then argues that people who believe they are experiencing pleasure merely because they are not in pain hold a false belief.
textual_basis: At 43e Socrates sets up three kinds with material metaphors (gold, silver, neither) and argues that the intermediate life cannot be correctly named pleasant or painful. At 44a he moves from how people speak to what they actually believe and then exposes the falsehood of assuming that absence of pain is equivalent to pleasure, positing that the nature of each (pleasure and non-pain) is distinct.
limits: This observation does not assert that the argument deploys a formal fallacy of false dichotomy; it notes the textual move of rejecting a conflation of two states. Does not assess whether the argument succeeds.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
feature_id: feature_candidate_071
feature_label: assent_chain
observation: Protarchus gives a series of short assents that build Socrates' argument from the threefold framework through the claim that the intermediate life cannot correctly be called pleasant or painful to the conclusion that people's belief about pleasure is false because pleasure and non-pain are distinct in nature.
textual_basis: The short responses at 43e-44a form a continuous chain: Protarchus assents to the threefold framework, agrees the intermediate life cannot be properly named, agrees that people speak that way, assents to the report that people think they are experiencing pleasure when not in pain, and assents to the claim that this belief is false.
limits: This observation records the formal assent pattern; it does not evaluate whether Protarchus' assents are logically sufficient or whether he is being led into contradiction.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
textual_basis: At 44a Socrates states that people hold a false belief about pleasure if the nature of each (pleasure and non-pain) is separate. Protarchus confirms that they are indeed separate. This draws a definitional boundary between two phenomena commonly treated as one.
limits: This observation does not claim that Socrates is defining pleasure itself here; the passage distinguishes pleasure from non-pain as distinct natures, not offering a full definition of either.
```

```yaml
textual_basis: Socrates states that it is not easy to comprehend the condition in question (48b), then explicitly proposes to tackle it precisely because of its obscurity to make future cases easier (48b), and Protarchus assents with 'you may speak' (48b), which constitutes an explicit procedural agreement to continue the inquiry.
limits: This observation records a procedural move; it does not assess whether the inquiry later succeeds or whether the topic is adequately clarified.
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_philebus_0200
source_work: Philebus
stephanus_span: 48b-48c
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/philebus.txt
  stephanus_span: 48b-48c
  start_marker: 48b
  end_marker: 48c
  start_char: 74422
  end_char: 75241
  text_sha256: 89eee83fdc2bae50983b50d64cf6541e5dbcb75abac9e1e53df3b43c14286d84
greek_terms: []
english_gloss: ""
feature_family: elenchus
feature_id: feature_candidate_071
feature_label: assent_chain
observation: Socrates builds a short assent chain linking three claims: (1) envy is a pain of the soul, (2) the envious person takes pleasure in the misfortunes of neighbors, (3) ignorance is an evil. Protarchus assents to each step without resistance.
textual_basis: At 48b Socrates asks whether envy is a pain of the soul (Protarchus: 'yes'), then states the envious person is pleased at neighbors' misfortunes (Protarchus: 'very much so,' 48c), then states ignorance is an evil (Protarchus assents). The sequence of brief questions and affirmations constitutes a rapid assent chain culminating in the pivot 'from these things, consider the nature of the ridiculous' (48c).
limits: This observation tracks the assent structure, not whether the premises about envy and ignorance are true or persuasive.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation: Socrates designates 'the ridiculous' (to geloion) as the target for examination, building toward it through a chain about envy and ignorance, and Protarchus explicitly prompts him to continue with 'only speak' (48c).
textual_basis: After securing assent that envy is a pain, that the envious feel pleasure at others' misfortunes, and that ignorance is an evil, Socrates says 'from these things, consider what nature the ridiculous has' (48c), marking to geloion as the definiendum for the coming inquiry. Protarchus signals readiness with 'only speak.'
limits: This records the marking of a definiendum; it does not claim that a definition is actually reached in this span.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
  Socrates introduces a group of thinkers he calls "the enemies of Philebus"

  from pains at 44b-44c.
textual_basis: >
  At 44b Socrates asks Protarchus whether he understands "the enemies of
  Philebus," and at 44b-44c identifies them as natural philosophers who "say
  that pleasures do not exist at all" and hold that pleasures are "all escapes
  from pains."
limits: >
  The group is not named individually; this is a collective prosopographic
  reference to an intellectual position rather than to specific historical

  this span.
```

```yaml
  Socrates advises Protarchus to treat the anti-hedonist thinkers "like certain
  prophets" at 44c, but immediately distinguishes their prophecy from craft:
  they prophesy "not by art but by a certain harshness of a not ignoble
  nature" at 44c.
textual_basis: >
  At 44c Socrates says to use the anti-hedonists like prophets, explicitly
  denying that their prophecy proceeds from techne and attributing it instead
  to a harshness of nature that is not ignoble.
limits: >
  This is an analogy to prophets, not a full craft analogy mapping a techne
  domain onto a target. The point is precisely that techne is absent; the
  comparison is to a non-craft mode of insight. The observation does not
  establish whether Socrates endorses or rejects the anti-hedonist position.
```

```yaml
observation: "Socrates secures Protarchus's rapid agreement to a sequence of premises: that forming opinion exists, that feeling pleasure exists, that there is an object of opinion, that there is something by which the one feeling pleasure feels it, and that neither the opining subject nor the enjoying subject ever loses the fact of really doing its activity, whether it does so correctly or not."
textual_basis: "At 37a-37b, Protarchus gives short assents to each of Socrates's premises in rapid succession, building a chain of agreed points before the asymmetry question is posed at 37b."
limits: "This records the assent pattern; it does not assess whether the premises are sound or whether the chain leads to a refutation."
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
  start_char: 75241
  end_char: 76074
  text_sha256: cd69aed2746ef1c512961c1b43b7bcc29243195ec61092b034d202aa7bede461
greek_terms:
  - τριχῇ τέμνειν
  - κατὰ χρήματα
  - κατὰ τὸ σῶμα
  - ἐν ταῖς ψυχαῖς
  - ἀρετῇ
english_gloss: "cut threefold; according to money; according to the body; in the souls; virtue"
feature_family: tripartite_division
feature_id: feature_candidate_2782
feature_label: tripartite_classification_of_subject
observation: |
  Socrates divides self-ignorance (to me gnothi seauton) into three kinds: those who overestimate their wealth, those who overestimate their bodily qualities, and those who overestimate their psychic virtue. The division is presented as exhaustive (kata tria, 48d) and ordered with explicitly increasing scope: "more" (pleious) err about the body, "by far the most" (polu de pleistoi) err about the soul (48e).
textual_basis: |
  At 48d Socrates instructs Protarchus to "cut it threefold" (trichē temnein) and asks whether those ignorant of themselves necessarily suffer this condition in three ways (kata tria). At 48e he enumerates: first (prōton men) kata chrēmata; then more (pleious de ge) kata to sōma; and by far the most (polu de pleistoi ge) peri to triton eidos to tōn en tais psychais, mistaken about aretē.
limits: |
  This observation records the explicit tripartite structure and its quantitative escalation. It does not claim this tripartition maps onto the Republic's tripartite soul or any other Platonic schema. It does not evaluate whether the classification is logically complete.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
  Socrates introduces self-ignorance as the contrary of the inscription's injunction, referring to the opposite condition as in no way knowing oneself.

  At 48d Socrates says that the contrary of the inscription is what is called in no way knowing oneself. The reference to the inscription marks the Delphic maxim without naming it directly.
```

```yaml
greek_terms:
  - πειρῶ δὲ αὐτὸ τοῦτο τριχῇ τέμνειν
  - οὐ γὰρ μὴ δυνατὸς ὦ
  - λέγεις δὴ δεῖν ἐμὲ τοῦτο διελέσθαι
english_gloss: "try to cut this very thing threefold; for I would not be capable; you say then that I must divide this"
feature_family: turn_geometry
feature_id: feature_candidate_720
feature_label: explicit_procedure_control
observation: |
  Socrates first invites Protarchus to perform the tripartite division himself ("try to cut this very thing threefold," 48d). When Protarchus pleads incapacity ("for I would not be capable," 48d), Socrates immediately assumes the task ("you say then that I must divide this now," 48d) and proceeds to deliver the full tripartite analysis at 48e without further input from Protarchus.
textual_basis: |
  At 48d: Socrates says peirō de auto touto trichēi temnein. Protarchus responds ou gar mē dynatos ō. Socrates then says legeis dē dein eme touto dielesthai ta nyn, to which Protarchus assents (legō, kai deomai ge pros tōi legein). Socrates then delivers the full enumeration at 48e in extended speech.
limits: |
  This records the turn-taking mechanism, not any claim about irony or pedagogical strategy. It does not assert that Protarchus is feigning incapacity or that Socrates intended to take over from the start.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation: The opponents propose a method for grasping the nature of any kind: to look toward the most extreme instances rather than the slight ones. This is illustrated with hardness, where one examines the hardest things rather than the barely-hard. The method is then applied to the kind of pleasure.
textual_basis: At 44e Socrates reports the opponents' procedure as a general method: if one wishes to see the nature of any kind, look to the extreme instances rather than the minimal ones. Protarchus agrees the answer should look to the primary instances in magnitude. Socrates immediately extends the method to the genus of pleasure.
limits: The observation does not assert that this method is correct; it reports a methodological proposal attributed by Socrates to the anonymous 'difficult' opponents.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_philebus_0217
source_work: Philebus
stephanus_span: 43a-43b
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/philebus.txt
  stephanus_span: 43a-43b
  start_marker: 43a
  end_marker: 43b
  start_char: 63779
  end_char: 64610
  text_sha256: 51134930fb07478948bf6ad738c6b56f2f2f032f098ccea3c12a95f8a4ab478f
greek_terms:
  - οἱ σοφοί
  - ἄνω τε καὶ κάτω ῥεῖ
  - ὑπεκστῆναι
  - φεύγειν
english_gloss: ""
feature_family: myth_demarcation
feature_id: feature_candidate_2793
feature_label: doctrine_set_aside
observation: |
  Socrates introduces the doctrine of the wise (hoi sophoi) — that everything always flows up and down — and then explicitly declines to engage it, announcing his intention to escape the argument (hupekstēnai ton logon) and flee from it (pheugein), before redirecting Protarchus to a new question.
textual_basis: |
  At 43a Socrates says "the wise say: everything always flows up and down" (aei gar hapanta anō te kai katō rhei). He then says "I want to withdraw from this advancing argument" (hupekstēnai ton logon) and "I intend to flee this way, and you flee with me" (tēide oun dianooumai pheugein, kai su moi sumpheuge). The language of withdrawal and flight marks a deliberate boundary: the flux doctrine is acknowledged as a philosophical tradition but excluded from the present inquiry.
limits: |
  This observation records Socrates's explicit setting-aside of a cited doctrine; it does not claim the flux doctrine is mythic in genre or equivalent to the poetic myths demarcated elsewhere. The doctrine is attributed to philosophic sources (hoi sophoi), not poets or myth-tellers.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_philebus_0218

  text_sha256: 51134930fb07478948bf6ad738c6b56f2f2f032f098ccea3c12a95f8a4ab478f
greek_terms:
  - αἰσθάνεται
  - λανθάνομεν
  - οὐ καλῶς ἡμῖν εἴρηται
english_gloss: ""
feature_family: elenchus
feature_id: feature_candidate_2794
feature_label: prior_claim_refuted_by_assent
observation: |
  Socrates secures Protarchus's assent that living things are largely unaware of the affections they undergo, then uses this assent to declare that an earlier claim was not well said — namely, that changes up and down produce pleasures and pains.
textual_basis: |
  At 43b Socrates asks whether every living thing perceives what it undergoes (aisthanetai to paschon), including growth and similar processes. Protarchus answers "the complete opposite: almost all such things escape our notice" (oligou gar ta ge toiauta lelēthe panth' hēmas). Socrates immediately responds: "then what was just now said was not well said by us — that changes occurring down and up produce pains and pleasures" (ou toinun kalōs hēmin eirētai to nundē rhēthen, hōs hai metabolai katō te kai anō gignomenai lupas te kai hēdonas apergazontai). The refutation follows the elenctic pattern: new premise, assent, contradiction with a prior thesis.
limits: |
  This observation records the formal refutation move at 43b; it does not identify which earlier passage contains the claim being refuted, nor does it assess whether the refutation is valid or complete. The observation is limited to the structure of the exchange within 43a-43b.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_philebus_0219

greek_terms:
  - ταῦτα μὲν τοίνυν οὕτως ἔστω
  - σὺ δ’ ἀπόκριναι
english_gloss: ""
feature_family: turn_geometry
feature_id: feature_candidate_720
feature_label: explicit_procedure_control
observation: |
  Socrates twice exerts procedural control over the discussion within this short span: first by announcing his intention to flee from one line of argument (43a), then by issuing a directive that closes the prior topic and commands Protarchus to answer a new question (43a-43b).
textual_basis: |
  At 43a Socrates says "I want to withdraw from this advancing argument" and "I intend to flee this way, and you, flee with me" (hupekstēnai ton logon … pheugein … kai su moi sumpheuge). Then at 43a-43b he declares "let these things be so, let us say to them" (tauta men toinun houtōs estō, phōmen pros toutous) and immediately follows with "but you, answer whether…" (su d' apokrinai poteron…). The combination of closure formula plus imperative redirection constitutes explicit procedural control: Socrates determines what is set aside and what is taken up next.
limits: |
  This observation records Socrates's procedural directives within 43a-43b. It does not claim that Protarchus resists or that the control is unusual for the dialogue. It merely identifies the textual moves by which Socrates manages the argument's direction.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
feature_family: definition_ladder
```

```yaml
feature_label: definition_by_dichotomous_contrast
observation: >
  Socrates introduces a classification of pleasures into two types: those that
  only seem to be pleasures but are not (at 51a) and those that are true
  pleasures (at 51b). He explicitly rejects the thesis that all pleasures are
  merely cessations of pains, using this rejection as the pivot into the
  dichotomous classification.
textual_basis: >
  At 51a Socrates states he is not persuaded by those who claim all pleasures
  are cessation of pains, and invokes witnesses for the existence of pleasures
  that seem to be but are not at all. Protarchus then asks at 51b which
  pleasures one would correctly suppose to be true, and Socrates responds with
  the contrasting class.
limits: >
  The observation notes the introduction of a pleasure dichotomy but does not
  assess whether the classification is exhaustive or whether the rejected thesis
  is fairly characterized.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_philebus_0228
source_work: Philebus
stephanus_span: 39e-40a
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/philebus.txt
  stephanus_span: 39e-40a
  start_marker: 39e
  end_marker: 40a
  start_char: 57439
  end_char: 58272
  text_sha256: edee3e6d7d357b7752961eef3641b25f0c1fec410456cbf4c987cf7b38fb81a9
greek_terms:
  - θεοφιλής
english_gloss: "god-beloved"
feature_family: definition_ladder
feature_id: feature_candidate_1164
feature_label: criterion_for_evaluative_classification
observation: "At 39e-40a Socrates introduces a criterion linking moral character to divine favor: the just, pious, and good man is god-beloved (theophiles), while the unjust and wholly bad man is the opposite. This is inserted immediately before the claim that everyone is filled with hopes (logoi and painted images), setting up an implicit standard by which the content of hopes may be evaluated."
textual_basis: "At 39e Socrates asks whether the just, pious, and altogether good man is not god-beloved (theophiles). Protarchus agrees ('ti mēn?'). Socrates then asks whether the unjust and altogether bad man is the opposite, and Protarchus again agrees. This pair of questions immediately follows the agreement that pleasures and pains can be true or false and that future-directed states are hopes, and immediately precedes the characterization of hopes as inner logoi and painted images. The theophiles criterion thus stands at the pivot between the analysis of true/false pleasures and the examination of the content of future-directed hopes."
limits: "This observation records a classification criterion introduced in the argument flow. It does not claim that this is a new definition of piety or justice; it notes only that the theophiles criterion is deployed as a classifying device that will bear on the evaluation of hopes. The observation does not address whether Socrates endorses this criterion or is using it dialectically."
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation: At 49a Socrates announces that a further dichotomous division must be made of the bad affliction just identified. He then executes the division at 49a-49b, splitting those who hold false self-opinion into two groups: those who are weak and unable to avenge mockery, whom he calls ridiculous, and those who are strong and able to avenge themselves, whom he calls fearful and hostile.
textual_basis: Socrates uses the explicit procedural formula "this must still be divided in two" at 49a, then applies the dichotomy at 49b by sorting the class according to the presence or absence of strength and power to retaliate, assigning a distinct evaluative term to each branch.
limits: This observation records the explicit dichotomous division procedure and its execution; it does not assess whether the division is exhaustive or correct.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_philebus_0232

  end_char: 73648
  text_sha256: ee2d1dee8431846b6205f01b08038aaf57aa5f8e47ccc4255af7635371ddc105

english_gloss: ""
feature_family: pleasure_pain_mixture
feature_id: feature_candidate_2813
feature_label: mixture_taxonomy_distinguished
observation: "Socrates distinguishes three kinds of pleasure-pain mixtures: (1) mixtures in common bodily affections, both surface and internal (47c), (2) mixtures where soul contributes opposite elements to body so that both enter one combined mixture (47c-47d), and (3) mixtures the soul itself takes as its own mixture (47d)."
textual_basis: "At 47c Socrates introduces mixed bodily affections using the language of common affections of the body itself, both surface and internal. At 47c-47d he describes the second kind where soul contributes opposite elements to body, resulting in one mixture. At 47d he identifies the remaining mixture as one the soul itself often takes as a mixture for itself, then asks Protarchus how to describe this."
limits: "This observation records the taxonomy as stated; it does not assess whether these three kinds exhaust the possibilities or whether the taxonomy is consistent with earlier passages in the dialogue."
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
feature_label: characteristic_catalogue_for_definiendum
observation: "Socrates recapitulates the catalogue of emotional states that he and Protarchus had earlier proposed as loci where mixed pleasures and pains would be found: anger (orge), longing (pothos), lament (threnos), fear (phobos), love (eros), emulation (zelos), and envy (phthonos), plus 'as many such as these.' This recapitulation at 50c confirms that the preceding analysis of mixture has been working through this enumerated list."
textual_basis: "At 50b-c Socrates explicitly recalls that 'we proposed' (prouethemetha) these passions as the domain in which they said they would find the mixtures now repeatedly discussed, and Protarchus confirms with a simple assent. The list serves as a retrospective catalogue that organizes the preceding argument's targets."
limits: "This observation records the recapitulation of the catalogue. It does not assess whether the analysis of each item has been completed or whether any items remain untreated. It does not claim that the list is exhaustive of all mixed states."
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
feature_id: feature_candidate_2839
feature_label: genesis_ousia_contrast
observation: At 53e-54a Socrates introduces a fundamental ontological distinction between two things: genesis (coming-to-be) of all things and ousia (being), as a second, distinct one.
textual_basis: At 54a Socrates says "let us take two different things" and specifies "one as genesis of all things, the other one as ousia." Protarchus accepts the pair, summarizing them as ousia and genesis. Socrates then asks whether genesis is for the sake of ousia or ousia for the sake of genesis.
limits: The passage posits the distinction but does not yet determine which is for the sake of which. The terms are not yet linked to the earlier pleasure/knowledge contest.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
  - οὗ χάριν
english_gloss: ""
feature_family: teleological_structure
feature_id: feature_candidate_2840
feature_label: for_the_sake_of_relation
observation: At 53e Socrates states that one class of beings always is for the sake of something (heneka tou), and the other is that for the sake of which (hou charin) each thing that comes to be for the sake of something always comes to be.
textual_basis: Socrates describes a two-term teleological structure: to men heneka tou ... est' aei (one is always for the sake of something), to d' hou charin ... gignetai (the other is that for the sake of which the thing coming to be for something comes to be). This sets up the question at 54a about whether genesis is for the sake of ousia or the reverse.
limits: This is a formal schema; the passage does not yet apply it to pleasure or knowledge. The terms leave open which relatum occupies which role.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation: Protarchus says at 53e that he has understood only with difficulty (mogis emathon) because the point was stated many times (dia to pollakis lechthenai), and Socrates at 54a addresses him as "child" (o pai) while deferring fuller understanding to later in the argument.
textual_basis: After Socrates' formulation of the for-the-sake-of relation, Protarchus responds mogis emathon dia to pollakis lechthenai (I barely understood through its being said many times). Socrates replies "perhaps, child, we will learn better as the argument proceeds" (tacha d' isos, o pai, mallon mathesometha proelthontos tou logou).
limits: This records a local dramatic moment of incomplete uptake, not a refutation or aporia. The observation does not claim Protarchus' difficulty is feigned or ironic.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
- γαργαλίζει

- συντείνει

- παντοῖα χρώματα
- παντοῖα σχήματα
- παντοῖα πνεύματα

english_gloss: ""
feature_family: pleasure_pain_mixture
feature_id: feature_candidate_2776
feature_label: catalog_of_mixed_emotions
observation: "Socrates describes the physiological and psychological effects of intense mixed pleasure-pain in a catalogue that lists tingling (gargalizein), mild distress, tension, leaping, varied colours, varied postures, varied breaths, shock/amazement (ekplexis), shouts, and folly (aphrosune) at 47a-47b. The catalogue is ordered as an intensifying sequence from mild bodily sensation to extreme loss of self-control."
textual_basis: "At 47a, Socrates opens the mixed-pleasure description with the tingling and mild-distress phase (gargalizein te kai erema aganaktein), then rises to tension and leaping (sunteinei ... pedan), and culminates in the multi-modal outburst catalogued with the threefold pantoiā series (colours, postures, breaths) and the terminal pair ekplexin kai boas meta aphrosunēs. The entire description runs through 47a and is approved by Protarchus at 47b (mala ge)."
limits: "This observation does not address whether the catalogue is meant to cover all mixed pleasures or only the most intense bodily kind. It records the catalogue structure, not a normative claim about the value of such pleasures."
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml

english_gloss: ""
feature_family: prosopography
feature_id: feature_candidate_1174
feature_label: ethical_type_profile
observation: "Socrates constructs a profile of the person devoted to intense mixed pleasures at 47a-47b: such a person says he is dying from the pleasure, pursues these pleasures the more the more intemperate (akolastoteros) and thoughtless (aphronesteros) he is, calls these the greatest pleasures, and ranks the person who lives most in them as happiest (eudaimonestaton). The profile is a composite ethical type, not a reference to any named individual."
textual_basis: "Socrates reports the self-description (legein ... hoion apothnēskei, 47b), links intensity of pursuit to degree of akolasia and aphrosunē (47b), and attributes the value-judgments 'greatest pleasures' and 'happiest' to this type (47b). Protarchus confirms at 47b that Socrates has captured the doxa of the many (tōn pollōn anthrōpōn)."
limits: "This observation records the construction of an ethical type; it does not assert that Socrates endorses or rejects the profile. The confirmatory remark by Protarchus attributes the opinion to the many, not to Socrates."
review_status: accepted
```

Socrates' description is thus explicitly framed as a report of popular opinion (doxa), not as a neutral phenomenological account."
```yaml
textual_basis: "Protarchus's line at 47b uses pros tōn pollōn anthrōpōn eis doxan, characterizing Socrates's preceding catalogue as an articulation of the opinion of the many. This is a closing formula for the description that began at 47a."
limits: "This observation does not determine whether the popular opinion is being set up for refutation or endorsement. It records only the explicit attribution of the preceding description to the doxa of the many."
```

```yaml
observation: >
  At 52d-e Socrates introduces a purity criterion for evaluating pleasures,
  opposing the pure and unmixed (to katharon te kai eilikrines) against the
  intense, the frequent, the great, and the impetuous (to sphodra te kai to
  poly kai to mega kai to itamon).
textual_basis: >
  Socrates asks whether with respect to truth one should call the pure and
  unmixed or the intense, frequent, great, and impetuous (52d). He then
  explains that the purpose is to examine whether pleasure and knowledge each
  contain a pure and an impure part so that each coming pure to judgment makes
  the judgment easier (52e).
limits: >
  The observation notes the introduction of a purity-versus-intensity
  criterion; it does not claim that the criterion is applied or that a
  definition of pleasure follows from it in this span.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
feature_label: criterion_for_evaluative_classification
observation: "Socrates proposes examining the genus of white and establishes a criterion for what counts as truest and most beautiful: purity defined as being unmixed, with no share of any other color, rather than the greatest or most extensive in quantity. Protarchus assents that what is most pure/unmixed is truest and most beautiful. Socrates then concludes that a small pure white is whiter, more beautiful, and truer than a large mixed white."
textual_basis: "At 53a, Socrates asks whether the purity of white is determined by magnitude and quantity or by being most unmixed with no share of any other color. Protarchus at 53a answers that it is what is most unmixed/pure. At 53b, Socrates concludes that a small pure white is whiter, more beautiful, and truer than a large mixed white."
limits: "This observation records that a criterion for evaluative classification (purity over magnitude) is established using a sensory paradigm; it does not claim that this criterion is ultimately endorsed in the dialogue or that it applies to the pleasure argument without further qualification."
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
feature_label: absurd_consequence_derived
observation: Socrates derives an absurd consequence from the hedonist position: if pleasure is the good, then one who chooses pleasure also chooses generation and destruction rather than the third life of pure thought without pleasure or pain.
textual_basis: At 55a, Socrates argues that if one chooses pleasure, one thereby also chooses genesis and phthora (coming-to-be and destruction), since pleasure belongs to the realm of becoming, not the third life of pure phronesis. Protarchus concedes the inference, then remarks that great absurdity follows if one posits pleasure as the good.
limits: This observation records the elenctic structure of the argument but does not assess whether the inference is valid or whether Socrates commits a fallacy.
review_status: accepted
```

He then launches a new argument at 55b about the absurdity of restricting all good to pleasure alone in the soul while excluding courage, moderation, and intelligence.

```yaml

greek_terms: []
english_gloss: ""
feature_family: craft_analogy
feature_id: feature_candidate_2851
feature_label: craft_precision_deficiency_test
observation: Socrates argues that if one separates arithmetic, measurement, and weighing from any craft, what remains is meager (phaulon), reducing the craft to conjecture (eikazein), sense-perception trained by experience (empeiria) and routine (tribe), and reliance on the powers of stochastic art.
textual_basis: At 55e Socrates states that stripping arithmetic, metretike, and statike from any techne leaves something phaulon; the remainder is conjecture and sense-perception cultivated by empeiria and tribe, using stochastic powers that many call technai (55e-56a).
limits: This observation records the precision criterion applied to crafts; it does not claim that Socrates rejects probabilistic crafts outright, nor does it assess the truth of the ranking.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
feature_label: music_as_stochastic_craft
observation: Socrates classifies music (mousike) and especially aulos-playing (auletike) as crafts filled with the unclear and possessing only a small amount of the stable, because they tune consonance by practiced conjecture rather than by measurement.
textual_basis: At 56a Socrates says music fits consonance not by measure but by the stochasmos of practice, and that the whole of auletike hunts the measure of each string by guessing, so that it holds much that is mixed with the unclear and only a little that is stable.
limits: The observation applies to the classification of music at 56a; it does not address other passages or evaluate whether music is genuinely a techne elsewhere in the dialogue.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml

feature_family: craft_analogy
feature_id: feature_candidate_2862
feature_label: craft_body_mixture_analogy
observation: Socrates describes states of pleasure-pain mixture using body-surface metaphors and technical vocabulary of itching (psora), tickling (gargalismoi), rubbing (tripsis), and scratching (knesis), mapping internal bodily conditions onto sensation outcomes through mixture language.
textual_basis: At 46d-46e Socrates classifies mixtures where pains exceed pleasures by naming specific bodily states: the so-called itches and tickles. When internal heat and inflammation are not reached by rubbing and scratching (knesis) but only the surface is diffused, people turn to fire or the opposite, producing intense pleasures, sometimes the reverse — pains mixed with pleasures. The passage deploys the language of mixture (meixeis, synkerastheisas, synkekrimena, diakekrimena) applied to somatic processes.
limits: This observation records the use of craft/body-analogy vocabulary applied to pleasure-pain mixtures. It does not claim that Socrates presents a developed medical theory, nor does it assert that the analogies resolve the dialogue's larger argument about the good life.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
feature_label: mixture_proportion_classification
observation: Socrates classifies pleasure-pain mixtures into those where the components are equal (ex ison) and those where one side predominates (ek ton heteron pleionon), and further subdivides by direction of predominance using the verb rhepo (to incline or tip the balance).
textual_basis: At 46d Socrates asks whether such mixtures are of equal pains and pleasures or of one side in greater proportion; Protarchus assents. At 46e, after describing a specific case where pains exceed pleasures, Socrates uses rhepo to describe how the mixture can incline toward either side depending on the body's condition. The classification schema (equal vs. unequal, direction of predominance) structures the analysis of mixed states.
limits: This observation records a proportional classification of mixed states within the passage. It does not claim that this is a formal definition or that the classification is exhaustive. It does not assess whether the schema is sustained throughout the dialogue.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
greek_terms: ["λόγος", "δόξαν"]
english_gloss: ""
feature_family: definition_ladder
feature_id: feature_candidate_490
feature_label: definition_proposed
observation: Socrates defines logos (spoken discourse) as the vocalization to another person of what was previously held internally as doxa (opinion or judgment).
textual_basis: At 38e, Socrates describes that if someone else is present, a person voices aloud (enteinas eis phonen) what was said to themselves (ta pros hauton rhethenata), and in this way logos comes into being from what was previously called doxa.
limits: This defines only the external-vocalization path from doxa to logos; the passage also notes the alternative case of solitary reflection in which no spoken logos arises.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
feature_id: feature_candidate_2881
feature_label: internal_dialogue_as_doxa_formation
observation: Socrates describes doxa as arising from an internal question-and-answer process in which a person asks themselves about an appearance and answers, the assertion then counting as a doxa whether correct or not.
textual_basis: At 38d, Socrates illustrates the mechanism: someone sees an indistinct figure (phantasthenta) under a tree and asks themselves what it is, then answers themselves (hôs apokrinomenos an pros hauton eipoi). This internal assertion is a doxa, and it may be correct (epitychôs) or mistaken (parenchtheis).
limits: The account uses a perceptual example of a distant human figure; it does not address doxa arising from non-perceptual sources.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation: "Socrates introduces a foundational two-fold distinction: one thing is 'itself by itself' (auto kath' hauto), the other is 'always desiring another' (aei ephiemenon allou). He then glosses the first as that which is most august by nature, the second as defective in relation to the first."
textual_basis: "At 53d Socrates states there are two kinds, the auto kath' hauto and the aei ephiemenon allou, then glosses the former as semnotaton aei pephukos and the latter as ellipes ekeinou. He proceeds to illustrate with the example of beautiful noble beloveds and their lovers."
limits: "This observation captures the introduction of the distinction and its initial gloss. It does not track how the distinction maps onto pleasure or genesis/ousia later in the argument."
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
feature_id: feature_candidate_2851
feature_label: craft_precision_deficiency_test
observation: Socrates proposes to divide crafts (technai) into two groups according to their precision, using the amount of measurement and instruments employed as the criterion. Crafts that follow music have less precision; those that follow building (tektōnikē) have more.
textual_basis: At 56b–56c Socrates says 'let us divide the so-called arts in two: those that accompany music in their works partake of less precision, those that accompany building partake of more' (56c). He previously cites medicine, farming, piloting, and generalship, then singles out building as using the most measures and instruments (56b), thus preparing the dichotomy.
limits: This observation registers the precision criterion for craft classification and the music/building dichotomy. It does not assess whether the ensuing examples (shipbuilding, housebuilding, woodwork) exhaust the high-precision group or whether the dichotomy is exhaustive.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_philebus_0284
source_work: Philebus
stephanus_span: 58b-58c
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/philebus.txt
  stephanus_span: 58b-58c
  start_marker: 58b
  end_marker: 58c
  start_char: 94135
  end_char: 94977
  text_sha256: 57c6de5eb2cfe64875d6518c1b87be7503d0702b013c92f05bedd3b46c3a6c81
greek_terms: ["τὸ σαφὲς", "τἀκριβὲς", "τὸ ἀληθέστατον", "μεγίστη", "ἀρίστη", "πλεῖστα ὠφελοῦσα"]
english_gloss: "the clear, the precise, the truest; greatest, best, most beneficial"
feature_family: definition_ladder
feature_id: feature_candidate_2903
feature_label: inquiry_target_refined
observation: Socrates corrects the target of the investigation at 58c: the question is not which craft is greatest, best, and most beneficial (megistē, aristē, pleista ōphelousa), but which craft examines the clear, the precise, and the truest (to saphes, takribes, to alēthestaton) — even if it is small and of little benefit.
textual_basis: At 58c Socrates explicitly denies that his earlier question sought the craft that "excels all in being the greatest and best and most beneficial to us" and states the real target: "which one examines the clear, the precise, and the truest, even if it is small and benefits little." This is a refinement of the inquiry's definiendum from a practical-excellence standard to an epistemic-purity standard.
limits: This observation records the refinement of the inquiry target within this passage. It does not claim that this refinement resolves the dialogue's overall search, nor does it assess whether the distinction holds across the full dialogue.
review_status: accepted
```

Protarchus's deferential reply confirms the correction lands, but Socrates then pivots at 58c to a substantive clarification.
```yaml
limits: This observation records the teasing dynamic at 58b. It does not claim to identify what specific metaphor Protarchus was avoiding, nor does it assess whether Socrates's detection is sincere or ironic.
```


```yaml
observation_id: obs_philebus_0290
source_work: Philebus

  text_sha256: 27979a5314115bab0e08976aeed23541650d328fb9e344c2949ccc74a15da9b5
greek_terms: ["βέβαιον", "καθαρὸν", "ἀληθὲς", "εἰλικρινές", "ἀμεικτότατα", "δεύτερά", "ὕστερα"]
english_gloss: ""
feature_family: forms_trajectory
feature_id: feature_candidate_1186
feature_label: stable_being_hierarchy
observation: |
  At 59c Socrates claims that stability (to bebaion), purity, truth, and what is called the unmixed (eilikrines) belong to things that are always in the same state according to the same unchanging condition — or to what is most akin to them — while everything else is to be ranked second and later.
textual_basis: |
  Socrates distinguishes a primary ontological class of unchanging beings (ta aei kata ta auta hosautos ameiktotata echonta, 59c) that ground certainty, purity, truth, and the unmixed; everything else is labelled second (deutera) and later (hystera). The hierarchy is asserted as a ranking principle (lekton, 59c), not argued for here, and Protarchus assents with full agreement.
limits: |
  This observation records a ranking claim, not an argument for the ranking. It does not address whether the second-tier items are sensible particulars, generated things, or mixed kinds. The text does not here specify what falls into each tier.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_philebus_0291
source_work: Philebus
stephanus_span: 59c-59d
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/philebus.txt
  stephanus_span: 59c-59d
  start_marker: 59c
  end_marker: 59d
  start_char: 96531
  end_char: 97305
  text_sha256: 27979a5314115bab0e08976aeed23541650d328fb9e344c2949ccc74a15da9b5
greek_terms: ["νοῦς", "φρόνησις", "κάλλιστα", "τιμήσειε"]
english_gloss: ""
feature_family: forms_trajectory
feature_id: feature_candidate_1117
feature_label: form_properties_catalogue
observation: |
  At 59c-d Socrates leads Protarchus through an assent chain that names nous and phronesis as the names (onomata) most worthy of honor, applies the finest names to the highest things, and places these names among conceptions (ennoiai) about what really is (to on ontos), where they are said to be exacted and correctly set.
textual_basis: |
  The short assent sequence runs: the finest names should be assigned to the finest things (59c); nous and phronesis are the names one would honor most (59d); these names are exacted (aperkribomena) and correctly set (orthos keimena) among conceptions concerning what really is (59d). Protarchus assents at each step. Socrates then confirms these are the very names he submitted for the earlier judgment (krisis, 59d).
limits: |
  The observation records a procedural use of an ontological vocabulary to rank noetic terms. It does not define nous or phronesis, does not determine whether they name forms or faculties, and does not settle whether the ranking is itself a form claim.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
textual_basis: |
  Socrates says: the names I then submitted for the judgment (krisis) are nothing other than these names (tauta ta onomata, 59d). Protarchus confirms. This is a speaker-level recapitulation that binds the current discussion of purity and honor to the earlier formal contest between phronesis and hedone.
limits: |
  Does not describe the earlier krisis in detail or specify its Stephanus location. Records only the recapitulation gesture, not its accuracy.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_philebus_0299
source_work: Philebus
stephanus_span: 59a-59b
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/philebus.txt
  stephanus_span: 59a-59b
  start_marker: 59a
  end_marker: 59b
  start_char: 95652
  end_char: 96531
  text_sha256: 97017981743fa95da8f262a2952155dbd2cafe67c3e2d30e1468dbdf6baf60fa
greek_terms: []
english_gloss: ""
feature_family: forms_trajectory
feature_id: feature_candidate_2265
feature_label: being_becoming_distinction_with_epistemic_correlates
observation: >
  Socrates contrasts two domains of inquiry: one concerning opinion (doxa) and another concerning
  nature, where the investigator studies this cosmos — how it came to be, what it undergoes, and what
  it does (59a). He then draws the distinction between "the things that always are" and "the things
  that are coming to be, will come to be, and have come to be" (59a). From this he infers that no
  clarity (saphes) according to the most exact truth can attach to things that never possess stability
  (59a-59b), and consequently that neither nous nor any episteme concerning them possesses the truest
  character (59b).
textual_basis: >
  At 59a Socrates asks whether the inquirer into nature studies opinion-like matters then immediately
  poses the contrast between "the things that always are" (ta onta aei) and the domain of becoming
  (ta gignomena kai genesomena kai gegonota). At 59b he introduces the epistemic corollary: nothing
  stable (bebaion) can come from unstable objects, so nous and episteme with the truest grasp cannot
  belong to them.
limits: >
  The observation records the being/becoming contrast and its epistemic consequence within 59a-59b.
  It does not assess whether this is the final position of the dialogue or whether the distinction
  is refined later.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation: >
  Protarchus responds to Socrates' series of four questions with minimal assents: "It is so" (houtos),
  "most true" (alethestata), "and how?" (kai pos), and "I think by no means" (oimai men oudamos),
  followed by "no, not likely" (oukoun eikos ge). Each response grants the premise without resistance
  or elaboration, creating a tight assent chain across 59a-59b.
textual_basis: >
  At 59a Protarchus says houtos in reply to the first question, then alethestata. At 59b he asks
  kai pos as a rhetorical question conceding the point, then oimai men oudamos, and finally oukoun
  eikos ge. The sequence shows unbroken, minimal agreement across a chain of premises leading to the
  epistemic conclusion.
limits: >
  The observation records the pattern of minimal assent responses in this segment. It does not claim
  that Protarchus always responds this way elsewhere or that he fully grasps the implications.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
  - τελεώτατον
english_gloss: "sufficient, most perfect"
feature_family: definition_ladder
feature_id: feature_candidate_1164
feature_label: criterion_for_evaluative_classification
observation: "Socrates defines the nature of the good as that which differs from all other things by complete self-sufficiency: whatever living being possesses it always, through the end, completely and in every way, needs nothing else in addition, and has the most perfect sufficiency (to hikanon teleotaton)."
textual_basis: "At 60b-c Socrates asks Protarchus to confirm an agreed point — that the nature of the good differs from other things in a specific way (ten tagathou diapherein physin tōde tōn allōn), then articulates the criterion at 60c: that in which this is present needs nothing else ever and has the most perfect sufficiency (to de hikanon teleōtaton echein)."
limits: "This observation records the self-sufficiency criterion stated for the good. It does not assess whether the criterion is justified, whether it exhausts what the good is, or how it relates to earlier formulations in the dialogue."
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
greek_terms:
  - τότε
  - νῦν
  - συνομολογοῖτο
english_gloss: "then, now, would be agreed together"
feature_family: turn_geometry
feature_id: feature_candidate_1124
feature_label: recapitulation_or_restart
observation: "Socrates recapitulates prior argumentative agreements and confirms their continuing force: he asks Protarchus whether the things said then (tote) are also what is the case now, and whether a further point was agreed both then and now (kai tote kai nyn synomologoito). He also recalls the methodological procedure of separating pleasure and prudence and testing each unmixed in a life."
textual_basis: "At 60b Socrates asks 'aren't these the things that were being said then, and are they?' (ou taut' estin te kai ēn ta tote legomena). At 60b-c he asks 'and wasn't this too agreed by us both then and now?' (kai tode kai tote kai nyn hēmin an synomologoito). At 60c he recalls the argumentative procedure of separating each unmixed into a life (tō logō epeirathēmen chōris hekateron hekaterou thentes eis ton bion)."
limits: "This observation records the explicit recapitulation and confirmation of prior agreements. It does not assess whether the recalled content is accurate, whether the agreements were actually reached earlier, or how this move advances the argument."
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
textual_basis: At 61c Socrates says that just as if they were wine-pourers with springs beside them, one might liken the spring of pleasure to honey and the spring of wisdom to a sober, wineless, austere, and healthy water, and that they must try to mix these as beautifully as possible.
limits: This observation identifies the craft image used to set up the method; it does not assess whether the analogy is sustained or successful.
```

```yaml
observation: Socrates rejects the proposal to mix all pleasure with all wisdom as unsafe and announces a safer opinion about how to proceed with the mixture.
textual_basis: At 61d, after Protarchus tentatively agrees that mixing all pleasure with all wisdom might achieve the best result, Socrates replies that this is not safe (ouk asphales), and that he can declare an opinion about how they might mix more safely (akindyndoteron).
limits: This observation notes the methodological safety hedge; it does not evaluate the subsequent method or whether the safer opinion is actually delivered.
```

```yaml
observation: "Socrates flags a methodological trap: calling a craft by one homonymous name establishes the opinion that it is one, even when the inquiry is distinguishing two kinds by clarity and purity."
textual_basis: "At 57b-57c Socrates recalls that one craft had been found clearer and another more obscure, then asks whether speaking of a craft as homonymous sets up the opinion that it is one before asking again about two. The same-name formulation marks a false unity problem."
limits: "This observation identifies the homonymy-generated one/many problem. It does not resolve which crafts are at issue or evaluate the correctness of the bipartition."
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation: "Socrates defines the most precise knowledge by reference to what is, what really is, and what is by nature always in the same condition (to on, to ontos, to kata tauton aei pephykos, 58a). This is framed as a knowledge that exceeds even the exact mathematical sciences just acknowledged at 57e."
textual_basis: "At 57e Protarchus has conceded that the arithmetic and measurement arts are most precise. Socrates immediately counters at 58a that the power of dialectic would reject any other candidate ranked before it, and he characterizes dialectic's proper domain with three ontological descriptors."
limits: "This observation registers the introduction of form-object language as a criterion ranking a type of knowledge above mathematical sciences. It does not assess whether the claim is consistent with other dialogues or whether the ontological descriptors refer to Platonic forms in the technical sense."
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation: "Socrates frames the ranking of kinds of knowledge as a competitive judgment among technai (arts/sciences). In 57e Protarchus is asked whether the mathematical sciences are 'most precise sciences' (epistemas akribeis malista), and at 58a dialectic is presented as a rival candidate that would reject any technê ranked before it."
textual_basis: "At 57e the language of epistemai and the superlative akribeis malista treats knowledge-domains as comparable crafts competing for a rank. At 58a the phrase 'he tou dialegesthai dunamis' (the power of dialectic) is personified as a competitor that would reject another technê judged superior."
limits: "This observation notes the craft-competition framing of knowledge-ranking. It does not claim that dialectic is literally a craft or that Socrates endorses the personification device beyond the immediate argumentative context."
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
  - μείγνυ
english_gloss: ""

feature_id: feature_candidate_1164
feature_label: criterion_for_evaluative_classification
observation: "Socrates applies an ethical-accompaniment criterion to sort pleasures into those admissible and those inadmissible for the best mixture: true and pure pleasures plus those that accompany health, moderation, and all virtue are to be mixed in; pleasures that accompany folly and other vice are to be excluded as irrational to include."
textual_basis: "At 63e Socrates instructs which pleasures to mix ('mignu'): the true and pure pleasures and those that accompany health, moderation, and all virtue — described as following virtue like attendants of a god. He then explicitly excludes pleasures that always accompany folly and other vice, calling their inclusion a great irrationality (alogia) for anyone wanting to see the finest and least factious mixture."
limits: "This observation records the classification criterion applied at this stage of the mixture procedure. It does not claim this is the final or only criterion in the Philebus, nor does it address whether the same criterion is applied elsewhere in the dialogue."
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation: |
  Socrates states the criterion by which any mixture becomes entirely valuable
  or entirely worthless: whether it attains measure and commensurate nature.
  Any combination lacking these necessarily destroys both its ingredients and
  itself, becoming not a true mixture but an un-mixed jumbled heap, a genuine
  disaster for its possessors (64d-64e).
textual_basis: |
  Socrates explicitly frames the passage as identifying the cause by which any
  mixture is judged entirely valuable or worthless, then states the
  measure-and-commensurability condition and the destructive consequence of its
  absence (64d-64e).
limits: |
  The observation records that a criterion for evaluative classification of
  mixtures is stated; it does not assess whether the criterion is applied
  consistently elsewhere or whether it exhausts the Philebus account of the good.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
  commensurability are identified as beauty and excellence occurring everywhere;
  and truth is confirmed as having been mixed in with these in the mixture
  (64d-64e).
textual_basis: |
  Three definitional moves are compressed into a short assent sequence: the
  good's power takes refuge in the beautiful's nature, measure and
  commensurability are equated with beauty and excellence, and truth is recalled
  as an additional ingredient in the mixture (64d-64e).
limits: |

  claim this is the final definition of the good in the Philebus or that the
  refuge metaphor resolves all earlier aporiai.
```

```yaml
feature_label: pleasure_shame_concealment_claim
observation: Protarchus contrasts nous and phronesis, which are never perceived as shameful, with the greatest pleasures, whose observers feel shame and hide them in darkness as if they ought not to be seen in the light.
textual_basis: At 65e-66a Protarchus argues that no one has ever seen phronesis or nous as aischron awake or in a dream, whereas the greatest pleasures (hedonas megistas) attract ridicule or the most shameful accompaniment, prompting people to hide them by night.
limits: This is a rhetorical flourish supporting the ranking, not an argumentative proof; it records a shame-based contrast between intellectual and pleasurable pursuits.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
  text_sha256: "1aeea8b89c76d4af7f9a814deeccab7d3e9ccd2d7465fa93c586d0331985d36f"
greek_terms: ["κάλλει", "συμμετρίᾳ", "ἀληθείᾳ", "τὸ ἀγαθὸν", "ἰδέᾳ"]
english_gloss: ""
feature_family: definition_ladder
feature_id: feature_candidate_1164
feature_label: criterion_for_evaluative_classification
observation: "Socrates states that the good cannot be captured by a single idea, and instead proposes a triad of criteria — beauty, symmetry, and truth — as what should be held responsible for the goodness of the mixture."
textual_basis: "At 65a Socrates says they cannot hunt the good with one idea (mē miai dynametha idea to agathon thēreusai) and instead take it with three: beauty, symmetry, and truth. This triad is then treated as the cause of goodness in the mixture."
limits: "This observation records the criterion structure; it does not assess whether the triad exhausts the good or whether this is Plato's final view."
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml

greek_terms:
  - ἀλήθειαν
  - ἀληθῶς
english_gloss: "truth, truly"
feature_family: definition_ladder
feature_id: feature_candidate_2974
feature_label: truth_as_condition_for_being
observation: |
  Socrates states that what does not have truth mixed with it could never truly come to be, nor would it be if it had come to be. Truth is made a necessary condition for genuine being or becoming.
textual_basis: |
  At 64b Socrates says: "that with which we do not mix truth could never truly come to be, nor would it be having come to be" (hoi me meixomen aletheian, ouk an pote touto alethos gignoito oud' an genomenon eie). Protarchus assents immediately.
limits: |
  The passage does not define truth; it only asserts that truth must be a component of any mixture that genuinely exists. The remark is preliminary to determining what in the mixture is most valuable.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
  Socrates describes their present position as standing at the vestibule (prothurois) of the good's dwelling, marking that they have reached the threshold of the inquiry's target but have not yet entered.
textual_basis: |
  At 64c Socrates asks whether they can say they are now standing at the vestibule of the dwelling of the good (epi men tois tou agathou nun ede prothurois ... tes oikeseos ephestanai). Protarchus agrees.
limits: |
  This spatial metaphor marks progress toward the definition of the good without yet claiming to have arrived at it. It does not specify what remains to be investigated.
```

```yaml
  Socrates frames the next phase of inquiry as identifying what is most honored (timiotaton) and most the cause (malist' aition) in the mixture that makes the disposition welcome to all. This prepares a ranked evaluation of mixture components.
textual_basis: |
  At 64c Socrates asks: "what in the mixture would seem to us to be most honored and at the same time most the cause of such a disposition having become welcome to all?" (ti deta en te summeixei timiotaton hama kai malist' aition...). The question sets up a comparison between pleasure and intellect.
limits: |
  The passage does not answer the question; it only announces the next step of the investigation. It does not yet establish the criterion for ranking.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
  - νοῦς
  - φρονήσεως
english_gloss: ""
feature_family: definition_ladder
feature_id: feature_candidate_1164
feature_label: criterion_for_evaluative_classification
observation: |
  Socrates proposes truth-orientation as the criterion for determining which form of knowledge or art is supreme. At 58d he calls for examining the soul's power that loves truth and does everything for its sake, then asks whether the pure part of intellect (nous) and wisdom (phronesis) possesses truth most, or whether another more authoritative knowledge must be sought.
textual_basis: |
  Socrates explicitly frames the inquiry around a power of the soul that "loves truth" (eran tou alethous) and acts for its sake, then poses a comparative question about whether nous and phronesis most possess truth. The criterion of truth-access is what will distinguish the supreme knowledge from others.
limits: |
  The observation records that truth is proposed as an evaluative criterion for ranking forms of knowledge; it does not analyze whether this criterion is defended elsewhere or how it relates to other criteria in the dialogue.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
feature_label: scope_delimitation
observation: |
  Socrates narrows the scope of the inquiry by distinguishing the truth-seeking knowledge from "the many crafts" (hai pollai technai). At 58e, immediately after Protarchus concedes that no other knowledge or craft cleaves to truth more than the one under discussion, Socrates introduces the contrast with the many crafts and those who labor at them.
textual_basis: |
  Socrates transitions at 58e to a contrastive move: having secured Protarchus's agreement about the truth-seeking knowledge, he begins to mark off "the many crafts" as a separate class whose relation to truth will presumably be examined differently.
limits: |
  This observation notes the scope-delimiting function of the contrast at 58e; it does not claim that the many crafts are explicitly evaluated in this segment, only that the distinction is introduced as a boundary for the discussion.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
  - μειγνύναι

feature_family: myth_demarcation
feature_id: feature_candidate_2982
feature_label: homeric_mixing_allusion
observation: |
  At 62d Socrates invokes Homeric imagery by sending all the rejected sciences to flow into "the mixing-vessel of Homer and very poetic receptacles." This is not a substantive myth but a comic literary allusion used to dismiss the non-essential crafts.
textual_basis: |
  The metaphor of a Homeric mixing-bowl (a compound unique to this passage) combined with the gesture of letting all rejected sciences "flow" into it marks the boundary between serious dialectical mixture and merely poetic or ornamental inclusion. The reference is literary, not mythic.
limits: |
  This does not constitute a full myth within the dialogue. It is a brief Homeric tag used for tone, not a mythic proof or extended narrative. The observation does not claim that Socrates is endorsing or rejecting Homer.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_philebus_0329

  text_sha256: 0dab95a60caec365972015028b96778b5a19996bc651fbfc23904c05fec53d38
greek_terms:
  - πάσας
  - ἁθρόας
  - πρώτας
  - ἀληθεῖς
english_gloss: "all," "wholesale/collectively," "first," "true"
feature_family: turn_geometry
feature_id: feature_candidate_720
feature_label: explicit_procedure_control
observation: |
  At 62d-e Socrates proposes a procedural step: instead of admitting all pleasures collectively, they should select only the true ones first, just as they had done for the sciences. Protarchus agrees that it makes a great difference for safety to release the true pleasures first. Then Socrates asks whether the necessary pleasures should be mixed in too, and Protarchus says yes.
textual_basis: |
  The repeated contrast between "all collectively" and "first the true ones" structures the dialectical procedure for mixing the good life. This mirrors the earlier treatment of sciences where all sciences were let in wholesale and then a selection by kind is proposed. The turn from wholesale admission to selection by truth-value is an explicit procedural move.
limits: |
  This observation is about the text's explicit procedural vocabulary. It does not claim that the procedure is actually followed through or that the mixture is successful.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
english_gloss: "necessary" and "must be mixed in together"
feature_family: definition_ladder
feature_id: feature_candidate_2984
feature_label: mixture_criterion_shifting
observation: |
  At 62e Socrates shifts the criterion for inclusion in the mixture from truth to necessity. After letting in the true pleasures first, he asks whether the necessary ones should also be mixed in, and Protarchus immediately agrees. The criteria for inclusion in the good-life mixture are being enumerated sequentially: first what is pure/true, then what is necessary.
textual_basis: |
  Socrates asks at 62e whether, if some pleasures are necessary, just as in the earlier case, these too must be mixed in. The phrase "just as in that case" refers back to the parallel with sciences, where necessary arts were also admitted. The criterion shifts from truth-selection to necessity, and Protarchus accepts this without argument.
limits: |
  This observation notes a shift in the explicit inclusion criterion. It does not claim that the criteria are inconsistent or that the final mixture is incoherent.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
textual_basis: "At 66c Socrates introduces the sixth rank with the formula 'hekta d' en genea, phesin Orpheus, katapausate kosmon aoides' and immediately applies the same verb katapauo to his own logos, marking a self-conscious closure device."
limits: "This records the Orphic citation as a mythic closure device. It does not claim that the ranking is Orphic doctrine or that the citation encodes esoteric meaning."
```

```yaml
textual_basis: "At 66d-e Socrates restates Philebus's original thesis that all and complete pleasure is the good, then his own counter-position that intelligence is far better and finer for human life, and finally his hedge that if something better than both appeared he would fight for second place for intelligence over pleasure. Protarchus confirms each recap at 66e."
limits: "This observation records the recapitulation structure only. It does not evaluate whether the final ranking is consistent with the recapped positions, nor does it interpret the hedge as irony or disingenuousness."

```

```yaml
observation: >
  At 61e Socrates recapitulates the earlier distinction between a knowledge
  that looks to things that come to be and perish and a knowledge that looks
  to things that neither come to be nor perish but always are in the same
  respect and manner, and restates the judgment that the latter is truer than
  the former.
textual_basis: >
  The Greek at 61e explicitly contrasts two kinds of knowledge by their
  objects: one oriented toward generated and perishing things, the other
  toward what is always ungenerated, imperishable, and self-identical. The
  comparative claim that the second is "truer" (alēthestera) than the first
  ties the epistemic hierarchy directly to the ontological contrast between
  becoming and being.
limits: >
  This observation records the restated distinction and the comparative
  truth-claim. It does not evaluate whether the distinction is justified, nor
  does it address the earlier development of this distinction elsewhere in
  the dialogue.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation: >
  At 61e-62a Socrates proposes a hypothetical procedure: mix the truest
  segments of each kind of knowledge and ask whether the resulting mixture
  suffices to produce the most choiceworthy life, or whether something else
  is still needed.
textual_basis: >
  Socrates explicitly frames a conditional procedural step ("if we should see
  the truest segments of each and mix them first, would these mixed together
  be sufficient for producing the most choiceworthy life…?"). Protarchus
  assents that this seems the right course. The question-structure is an
  explicit invitation to test a compositional hypothesis rather than to state
  a settled conclusion.
limits: >
  The observation records the hypothetical-procedure structure and the
  compositional (mixing) metaphor. It does not assess whether the mixing
  procedure succeeds or whether the life in question is actually attained
  later in the dialogue.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
  At 62a-62b Socrates asks whether someone with the account of the divine
  circle and sphere itself, but ignorant of the human sphere and these circles
  used in building and related practices, would have knowledge sufficiently.
```

```yaml
greek_terms: []
english_gloss: ""
feature_family: craft_analogy
feature_id: feature_candidate_3027
feature_label: animal_witness_as_criterion
observation: "Socrates draws a contrast between two kinds of witness for judging pleasures: the pursuit of pleasure by all beasts (cattle, horses, and all other animals) versus the reasoned accounts of those engaged in the philosophical Muse. The many trust the former as if relying on bird-augury."
textual_basis: "At 67b Socrates says that not even if all cattle, horses, and every beast affirm the pursuit of pleasure should we trust them; the many use them like augurs trust birds (hosper manteis ornisin), judging pleasures best for living well, and regard the lusts of beasts as more authoritative witnesses than the logoi of those who practice the philosophical Muse."
limits: "This observation records the contrast structure and the craft-analogy language of augury (mantis/ornis), not any claim about what Socrates secretly thinks about animal-witness."
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
  Socrates instructs Protarchus to look toward three things — intellect, truth, and pleasure — and answer which is more akin to truth (65c). observation_id: obs_philebus_0354
```

```yaml
  - πρός τι
  - εἴδη
english_gloss: ""
feature_family: forms_trajectory
feature_id: feature_candidate_4690
feature_label: intrinsic_vs_relative_property_contrast
observation: At 51c Socrates distinguishes shapes that are beautiful "by themselves" from those that are beautiful "relative to something," claiming straight, circular, and ruler-compass-generated plane and solid shapes possess their own proper pleasures incomparable to the pleasures of scratching. The same intrinsic-vs-relative criterion is then applied to colors, sounds, and smells across 51c-51e.
textual_basis: At 51c Socrates says these shapes are not beautiful "relative to something" but are always beautiful "by themselves" and have pleasures proper to them, contrasting them with pleasures of scratching. At 51d the criterion extends to colors; at 51d-51e to smooth and bright sounds that emit a single pure melody; at 51e to smells that are not mixed with necessary pains. At 51e Socrates calls these "two kinds" of pleasures.
limits: The passage lists categories but does not fully define what makes a pleasure pure; the intrinsic-vs-relative contrast is stated as a criterion, not argued for.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_philebus_0357
source_work: Philebus
stephanus_span: 56d-57a
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/philebus.txt
  stephanus_span: 56d-57a
  start_marker: 56d
  end_marker: 57a
  start_char: 90969
  end_char: 92248
  text_sha256: 2bb31105d1a32216646d8e240f60d5cc15ddabb11e693ba9064b0e7fd94e3ab0
greek_terms:
  - ἀριθμητική
  - μονάδας
  - λογιστικὴ
  - μετρητικὴ
  - γεωμετρίας
english_gloss: ""
feature_family: craft_analogy
feature_id: feature_candidate_4692
feature_label: craft_domain_split
observation: >
  Socrates successively distinguishes two kinds within each mathematical art
  at 56d-57a. First, at 56d-56e, he separates popular arithmetic (which counts
  unequal units such as two armies or two oxen) from philosophical arithmetic
  (which requires every unit to be exactly equal to every other). Then, at
  56e-57a, he extends the same split to calculation and measurement: the kind
  used in carpentry and commerce versus the kind pursued in philosophy
  (geometry and calculation). Protarchus agrees that each pair should be
  divided into two.
textual_basis: >
  At 56d Socrates asks whether arithmetic is not one kind for the many and
  another for philosophers. At 56e he explains that ordinary arithmeticians
  count unequal units while philosophers insist on strict unit-equality.
  Protarchus agrees the difference is significant. At 56e-57a Socrates poses
  the same question for calculation and measurement, contrasting carpentry
  and commerce with philosophical geometry and calculation, and Protarchus
  votes for dividing each into two.
limits: >
  The observation records the two-fold divisions and their agreement; it does
  not claim anything about which kind of pleasure the analogue will ultimately
  support.
review_status: accepted
```
